Good. Full market picture. Let me synthesize everything into one clean architectural brief.

THE MARKET

Existing players: OwnBackup (acquired by Salesforce, rebranded as Salesforce Backup & Recover at $10/user/month), GRAX (enterprise, BYOC AWS, complex), Odaseva (Fortune 500 only, sales-call required), DataArchiva (AppExchange-listed, adequate but low mindshare). Pricing ranges from $2.40/user/month (Metallic) to $17.5k–$235k/year (AutoRABIT), all targeting enterprise. 
DevOps Launchpad

The gap nobody fills well: self-serve, affordable, mid-market (50–500 SF users), pure storage-cost-reduction focus, no sales call required to start.

REAL PAIN POINTS (confirmed)

Archiving 10 GB to external storage can lower costs from $3,750/month to $0.23 — the delta is the pitch. Salesforce's own backup starts at $10/user/month, meaning 100 users = $12k/year just for backup, before even touching storage overages. Your product replaces both at a fraction of the price. 
Skyvia
DataArchiva

Competitor weaknesses: all require sales calls or demos for pricing, none are self-serve, setup is weeks not minutes, minimum contracts are $10k+/year. Mid-market admins bounce.

WHAT YOU'RE BUILDING

A self-serve Salesforce storage optimizer. Admin signs up, connects org in 10 minutes, sees exactly what's eating storage and what it costs, starts archiving. Pays by usage. No sales call, no annual contract, no enterprise fluff.

FEATURES (PHASED)

Phase 1 — Core value, shippable in 6–8 weeks:

Storage analyzer: connect org → instant breakdown by object type showing exact GB and estimated monthly cost
Archive jobs: filter by object (Case/Opportunity), status, date range, record type, specific IDs → preview count + savings estimate → trigger
Real-time job progress (record level)
View archived content from your dashboard (HTML rendered from R2)
Metered billing via Stripe
Phase 2 — After first paying clients:

Managed package with LWC: inline archived content viewer on Case/Opportunity page, archive/restore trigger from within SF
Scheduled auto-archive (set-and-forget rules)
Restore to Salesforce (full fidelity with audit fields)
GDPR erasure support
Phase 3 — Enterprise wedge:

BYOC (client brings own R2/S3)
Multi-org per tenant
Webhook + Slack notifications
Audit export (CSV/PDF)
Data residency options (EU R2 buckets)
WHAT YOU ACTUALLY ARCHIVE AND HOW

EmailMessage: full delete + full JSON backup to R2 (bodies, headers, metadata, attachments). On restore: Bulk API Insert with Set Audit Fields upon Record Creation — client enables this one org setting during onboarding, then full fidelity is preserved. Your company already does this exact flow.

ContentVersion: delete binary, store in R2 with metadata JSON. Restore via insert + ContentDocumentLink.

FeedItem: archive as NDJSON per parent record. Phase 2+.

Salesforce (Phase 2)
├── Minimal managed package — LWC viewer + LWC archive trigger
├── Connected App           — OAuth

AUTH STRATEGY

Your platform login: email/password + Google OAuth (Lucia or NextAuth). Roles: superadmin (you), owner, admin, member per tenant.

SF OAuth: Web Server flow (Authorization Code + PKCE) for onboarding — identical to Google/GitHub login flow. Returns access_token + refresh_token → stored encrypted in D1. All background jobs use the refresh_token to get fresh access_tokens. No JWT Bearer complexity needed.

   □ Assign Permission Set to connected user

MANAGED PACKAGE REALITY

Phase 1: zero SF deployment. Admin triggers everything from your Next.js dashboard. Metadata API deploys custom fields automatically on connect. This ships in weeks not months.

Phase 2: minimal LWC package. One component file, no Apex. Deployed via install URL (one click). You push updates transparently — client never reinstalls. This is not "managed package with AppExchange review" — it's just a deployable LWC.

AppExchange listing: skip for first 10 clients. Validate PMF, get revenue, then decide if the 4–8 week security review is worth distribution. For mid-market, direct install URL works fine — they're not blocking unreviewed packages.

BOTTLENECKS TO PLAN FOR

SF API governor limits: Bulk API 2.0 handles this — designed for 50M+ record jobs. Your workflow must poll SF bulk job status, not run synchronously.

CF Workflow step limits: design each step to be idempotent. D1 is your state — if a workflow restarts, it reads D1 to know exactly where it left off, skips completed records.

Large attachments: ContentVersion binaries can be 100MB+. Stream directly from SF API to R2 in chunks — never load full binary in Worker memory.

Rate limits on SF REST API (used for display/cache): cap display queries at 5min TTL in D1 cache. Preview count uses a COUNT SOQL — cheap, fast.

Multi-tenant isolation: every D1 query, every R2 path, every Hono middleware call scoped to tenant_id. Enforce at middleware layer, not per-route.

Claude responded: Let me read the current code carefully, then think from scratch.
Let me read the current code carefully, then think from scratch.

What the current code actually does (observed, not assumed):

Single tenant. Python Lambda. Username/password OAuth (cached in S3). Step Functions orchestrating 150 parallel Lambdas. Composite API for fetching. Parallel streaming attachments to S3. Deletes EmailMessage + ContentVersion from SF. Archives bodies to JSON + TXT files. Updates custom fields on Case/Opportunity. Progress tracked via a custom SF object (Archival_Progress__c).

Real problems I see in this code:

Username/password flow — insecure, breaks on MFA, breaks on password rotation. Token cached in S3 as plaintext. No tenant isolation at all (hardcoded bucket, hardcoded creds). email.json is a flat file appended per-case — race conditions guaranteed under parallelism. append_metadata does get → mutate → put — classic read-modify-write race with 150 parallel workers. No idempotency — if a Lambda crashes after delete but before status update, data is gone with no recovery path. Progress is tracked in Salesforce itself, not in your system — you have zero visibility. FeedItems/FeedComments archived but never deleted from SF (inconsistent). MAX_QUERY_LENGTH = 2000 hardcoded string length hack. Composite API limit of 25 subrequests means you're batching manually all over the place with fragile index math.

RETHINKING FROM SCRATCH

What problem are we actually solving?

Salesforce storage is expensive. File attachments (ContentVersion) and email bodies (EmailMessage.HtmlBody) are the primary culprits. Customers need them deleted from SF to reclaim storage, but still accessible for compliance and user viewing. The current solution works for one tenant. We want to productize this for many tenants.

CORE ARCHITECTURAL DECISIONS

1. Replace username/password with OAuth Web Server flow

Store access_token + refresh_token per org in your DB. Refresh automatically. No plaintext passwords anywhere.

2. Replace S3 flat file accumulation with structured storage

Current approach: append to email.json per case — fragile under concurrency. New approach: one object per record in R2/S3, keyed by SF record ID. No merging, no read-modify-write, no races.

/{tenant_id}/
  /Case/{case_id}/
    /emails/{email_message_id}.json     ← one file per email, immutable
    /attachments/{content_version_id}   ← raw binary
    /attachments/{content_version_id}.meta.json
    /feeditems.json                      ← append-only, written once per job
  /Opportunity/{opp_id}/
    ...
3. Replace Step Functions + 150 Lambdas with Cloudflare Workflows

Step Functions with 150 parallel Lambdas is expensive, operationally complex, and has the glitch you've already experienced. Cloudflare Workflows give you durable execution, automatic retry, step-level checkpointing, and zero infrastructure management. Each step is idempotent by design.

4. Replace SF-side progress tracking with DB-side

Archival_Progress__c in SF is your source of truth right now. That's backwards. Your DB should own job state. SF gets updated as a side effect, not as the source of truth.

5. Bulk API 2.0 for large jobs, Composite API for small jobs

Current code uses Composite API + REST for everything. For jobs over ~500 records, Bulk API 2.0 is the right tool — async, no timeout risk, handles millions of records. For small on-demand jobs (<500 records), REST Composite is fine.

FULL SYSTEM DESIGN

ARCHIVE WORKFLOW (DETAILED, CORRECT)

ArchiveWorkflow triggered with: { job_id, org_id, tenant_id, object_type, filters }

Step 1 — CLAIM
  Read job from D1 → verify status = 'queued'
  Set status = 'running'
  Idempotency: if already 'running' or 'done', exit early

Step 2 — COUNT
  REST API: SELECT COUNT() FROM Case WHERE {filters}
  Write total_records to D1 jobs table
  Update SF: Case.keeper_cloud__Archival_Status__c = 'Pending'
  on all matching IDs (Bulk API 2.0 Update)
  Idempotency: skip if already written

Step 3 — FETCH IDs
  Bulk API 2.0 Query Job:
  SELECT Id FROM Case WHERE {filters}
  → get all matching IDs as CSV chunks
  Write each ID to D1 job_records with status = 'pending'
  (INSERT OR IGNORE for idempotency)

Step 4 — PROCESS RECORDS (chunked, resumable)
  For each chunk of 20 Case IDs from D1 where status = 'pending':
  
  4a. Composite API call (5 subrequests):
      - GET Cases metadata
      - GET EmailMessages for these cases  
      - GET EmailMessages with body fields (for archival)
      - GET FeedItems for these cases
      - GET FeedComments for these cases

  4b. For each EmailMessage:
      - Write /{tenant}/{Case}/{case_id}/emails/{email_id}.json to R2
        (one file per email, PUT is idempotent)
      - Compute SHA256, store checksum in D1 job_records

  4c. Parallel: for each ContentVersion/Attachment:
      - Stream binary from SF → R2 (one file per attachment)
        key: /{tenant}/{Case}/{case_id}/attachments/{cv_id}
      - Write metadata JSON alongside it
      - Idempotency: check D1 before downloading

  4d. Write FeedItems + FeedComments as JSON to R2
      (keyed by case_id, idempotent PUT)

  4e. VERIFY: count D1 archived == expected for this chunk
      If mismatch → mark chunk records failed, continue to next chunk
      Do NOT stop the entire job

  4f. If verification passes:
      - DELETE EmailMessages from SF (Composite, batch of 25)
      - DELETE ContentVersions from SF (Composite)
      - Update D1 job_records: status = 'archived'
      - Update SF: Case.keeper_cloud__Archival_Status__c = 'Archived'

  4g. Update D1 jobs.processed_records += chunk size
      (frontend polls this for progress bar)

Step 5 — FINALIZE
  Count D1 job_records where status = 'failed'
  Update D1 jobs: status = 'done', completed_at, failed_records count
  Update SF Archival_Progress__c: Status = 'Completed'
  Emit billing_events row (record_count for Stripe metered usage)
  Send email notification
  If failed_records > 0:
    Revert SF status to 'Pending' on failed records only
Key improvement over current code: each step reads D1 to know what's already done. If the workflow crashes at step 4f and restarts, it re-reads D1, sees which records are already archived, skips them. Zero double-delete risk.

R2 STORAGE STRUCTURE

/{tenant_id}/
  /Case/
    /{case_id}/
      /emails/
        /{email_message_id}.json     ← full EmailMessage object
      /attachments/
        /{content_version_id}        ← raw binary
        /{content_version_id}.meta.json  ← filename, mime, size, parent
      /feed/
        /feed_items.json             ← array, written once per job
        /feed_comments.json
  /Opportunity/
    /{opp_id}/
      ... same structure
No more flat email.json files that multiple workers race to update. Every write is a PUT to a unique key — idempotent, parallelism-safe.

D1 SCHEMA

sql
CREATE TABLE users (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,
  name TEXT,
  role TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'user',  -- 'superadmin' | 'user'
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE tenants (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,          -- globally unique slug
  owner_user_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id),
  stripe_customer_id TEXT,
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE tenant_members (
  tenant_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES tenants(id),
  user_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id),
  role TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'member',
  PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, user_id)
);

CREATE TABLE org_requests (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  tenant_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES tenants(id),
  requested_by TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id),
  org_name TEXT NOT NULL,
  sf_type TEXT NOT NULL,              -- 'production' | 'sandbox'
  status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'pending',
  meta_json TEXT,
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE sf_orgs (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  tenant_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES tenants(id),
  sf_org_id TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,
  sf_instance_url TEXT NOT NULL,
  sf_type TEXT NOT NULL,
  label TEXT NOT NULL,
  setup_complete INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE sf_tokens (
  org_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES sf_orgs(id),
  access_token TEXT NOT NULL,         -- AES-256 encrypted at rest
  refresh_token TEXT NOT NULL,
  sf_user_id TEXT NOT NULL,
  expires_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE jobs (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  org_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES sf_orgs(id),
  tenant_id TEXT NOT NULL,
  type TEXT NOT NULL,                 -- 'archive' | 'restore'
  sf_object_type TEXT NOT NULL,       -- 'Case' | 'Opportunity'
  filter_json TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'queued',  -- queued|running|done|failed
  total_records INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
  processed_records INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
  failed_records INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
  triggered_by_user_id TEXT REFERENCES users(id),
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL,
  completed_at INTEGER
);

CREATE TABLE job_records (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  job_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES jobs(id),
  sf_record_id TEXT NOT NULL,         -- Case ID or Opportunity ID
  status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'pending',  -- pending|archived|restored|failed
  r2_prefix TEXT,                     -- /{tenant}/{Case}/{record_id}/
  checksum TEXT,                      -- SHA256 of email bodies combined
  error TEXT,
  updated_at INTEGER NOT NULL,
  UNIQUE(job_id, sf_record_id)
);

CREATE TABLE audit_log (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  tenant_id TEXT NOT NULL,
  org_id TEXT NOT NULL,
  job_id TEXT,
  action TEXT NOT NULL,
  actor_user_id TEXT,
  record_count INTEGER,
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE billing_events (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  tenant_id TEXT NOT NULL,
  org_id TEXT NOT NULL,
  event_type TEXT NOT NULL,           -- 'archive' | 'restore'
  record_count INTEGER NOT NULL,
  reported_to_stripe INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE sf_data_cache (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,                -- org_id + ':' + cache_key
  org_id TEXT NOT NULL,
  data_json TEXT NOT NULL,
  fetched_at INTEGER NOT NULL,
  ttl_seconds INTEGER NOT NULL
);

CREATE INDEX idx_jobs_org ON jobs(org_id);
CREATE INDEX idx_job_records_job ON job_records(job_id);
CREATE INDEX idx_job_records_status ON job_records(job_id, status);
CREATE INDEX idx_audit_tenant ON audit_log(tenant_id);
CREATE INDEX idx_billing_unreported ON billing_events(reported_to_stripe);

Good. I have everything needed. Let me now think purely from first principles.

WHAT IS SALESFORCE AND WHY IS STORAGE A PROBLEM

Salesforce is a cloud CRM. It stores two types of data:

Data Storage — structured records: Cases, Opportunities, Contacts, EmailMessage records, custom objects. Each EmailMessage record = ~6–10KB minimum. A company with 1M historical emails = 6–10GB just in data storage. Additional data storage costs $125/month per 500MB, so 10GB of email records alone = $2,500/month in overages. 
DataArchiva

File Storage — binaries: attachments, PDFs, images stored via ContentVersion/ContentDocument objects. Extra file storage costs ~$5/GB per month. A company with 100GB of historical case attachments = $500/month extra. 
XfilesPro

A healthcare company was paying over $45,000 annually in file storage overages from scanned PDFs stored in Salesforce. Moving them externally reduced file storage by 92%. 
2-data

The root cause: Salesforce was never designed for long-term storage. Companies use it as a long-term system of record because they don't know better, and it gets expensive fast.

SALESFORCE API LANDSCAPE (FROM ZERO KNOWLEDGE)

There are multiple APIs, each for different use cases:

REST API — synchronous, standard CRUD. Good for up to ~2,000 records. 10-minute timeout hard limit. Governance: 100,000 + (1,000 × license count) requests per rolling 24-hour window for Enterprise. 
Unified.to

Composite API — bundles up to 25 REST subrequests into one HTTP call, counts as one API call. Reduces API consumption significantly for chatty integrations. Best for read-heavy operations fetching related objects together. 
Thesalesforcedev

Bulk API 2.0 — fully async, designed for 2,000+ records, up to millions. Query jobs don't consume the 15,000 batch/day limit — only ingest jobs do. Any operation over 2,000 records should use Bulk API 2.0. No timeout risk. SF processes in background, you poll for results. 
Salesforce

Metadata API — deploy/modify org configuration: create custom fields, page layouts, objects. This is how you programmatically set up a new client's org without a managed package install.

SALESFORCE AUTH (2025 REALITY)

In September 2025, Salesforce eliminated OAuth Device Flow completely and now blocks uninstalled Connected Apps for most users. This kills username/password flows for new integrations. 
Concretio

What works in 2026:

OAuth Web Server Flow (Authorization Code + PKCE) — user clicks "Connect", gets redirected to SF login, approves, you get access_token + refresh_token. This is the Google/GitHub OAuth pattern. Best for onboarding. Returns a refresh_token that survives long-term.

Client Credentials Flow — server-to-server, no user interaction. Requires a dedicated integration user assigned to the Connected App in SF setup. Best practice: one Connected App per integration, one dedicated integration user assigned via permission set. This ensures auditability and least-privilege access. 
Salesforce Developers

The right approach for our product: Web Server flow for onboarding (user connects their org interactively). Client Credentials flow for all background processing (archive/restore jobs run as dedicated integration user).

As of September 2025, a Salesforce admin must formally install the Connected App in their org before users can authorize it. One admin with "Approve Uninstalled Connected Apps" permission connects first, installs the app, then all subsequent users can connect normally. This means our onboarding must guide the admin through this one-time install step. 
Salesintel

WHAT EATS SALESFORCE STORAGE — THE REAL BREAKDOWN

Two separate buckets, two separate problems:

Data Storage culprits:

EmailMessage records: 6–10KB each. 1M emails = 6–10GB = $1,500–$2,500/month extra
FeedItem (Chatter posts): smaller but numerous
Task/Activity records: 2KB each but millions exist in active orgs
File Storage culprits:

ContentVersion (Salesforce Files system): PDFs, images, contracts — up to 2GB per file
Legacy Attachment object: older orgs still use this
Email attachments stored as ContentVersion linked via ContentDocumentLink
Key insight: These are different storage buckets with different pricing. A solution that only handles one is incomplete. We handle both.

WHAT ACTUALLY GETS ARCHIVED (THE PRODUCT DEFINITION)

From a user perspective, they think in terms of business objects: "I want to archive old Cases" or "Archive closed Opportunities." They don't think about EmailMessage or ContentVersion — those are implementation details.

When a user says "archive Cases closed before 2023":

Under the hood, for each Case we archive:

All EmailMessage records linked to that Case (data storage → R2)
All ContentVersion/Attachment binaries linked to that Case (file storage → R2)
All FeedItem/FeedComment records if desired (data storage → R2)
A snapshot of the Case metadata itself (for restore reference)
Then delete them from SF to reclaim storage.

The Case record itself stays in SF (it's tiny, 2KB). The bulk of storage is in the children.

THE RESTORATION PROBLEM

Restoring EmailMessage with original audit fields (CreatedDate, FromAddress, MessageDate) requires the org to have "Set Audit Fields upon Record Creation" enabled in User Management Settings, and the integration user must have that permission assigned. This is a one-time admin action during onboarding. Without it, restored emails show today's date instead of the original — unacceptable for compliance use cases.

ContentVersion restore = insert new ContentVersion + create new ContentDocumentLink. Standard permissions, no special flags needed.

Enterprise tools like AutoRABIT range from $17.5k to $235k/year. Veeam charges $3.60–$4/user/month for archiving. Salesforce's own backup starts at $10/user/month — 100 users = $12k/year just for backup, before storage overages.

Self-serve (no sales call to start)
Under $200/month for a real mid-market org

That is the gap.

FULL PRODUCT DEFINITION FROM SCRATCH

Name: (your choice — let's call it "Vault" for clarity)

What it is: Self-serve Salesforce storage optimizer. Connect your org, see what's costing you money, archive it to private cloud storage, access it natively, restore anytime.

Who it's for: Salesforce admins at companies with 50–1,000 users on Service Cloud or Sales Cloud who are hitting storage limits or paying overages. Specifically: companies with high Case volume (support teams) or high Opportunity attachment volume (sales teams).

FEATURES

1. Storage Analyzer

Connect org → instant breakdown of storage by object type
Shows: "EmailMessage: 8.2GB data storage = $2,050/month overage. Case Attachments: 45GB file storage = $225/month overage."
Projected savings if you archive everything older than X months
"You could save $2,275/month. Vault costs $149/month. Net savings: $2,126/month."
This feature alone is a sales tool
2. Archive Jobs

Object type selector: Case / Opportunity (user-facing, not EmailMessage)
Filters: status (closed/open), date range (created before / closed before), record type, case owner, specific IDs (comma-separated)
Preview: runs COUNT query, shows "4,821 Cases matched. Contains ~32,000 emails, ~8,400 attachments. Estimated storage freed: 12.4GB. Est. monthly savings: $310."
One-click "Start Archive" → job queued
Real-time progress: records processed, success/fail counts, ETA
3. Archived Content Viewer

Phase 1: inside your dashboard, search by Case/Opportunity ID → see all archived emails rendered as HTML, attachment download list
Phase 2: inside Salesforce, LWC component on Case/Opportunity page layout shows archived content inline — user never leaves SF
4. Restore

Single record restore: from viewer, one click
Bulk restore: filter by job, date, case ID → restore all
Full fidelity: original dates, senders, threading preserved (requires one SF org setting enabled during onboarding)
5. Scheduled Auto-Archive

"Archive all Cases closed for more than 6 months, every Sunday at 2am"
Configurable per-org, per-object, per-filter
Fully automated, no human trigger needed
6. Audit Log

Every action logged: who triggered, when, how many records, success/failure count
Exportable CSV for compliance
GDPR erasure: find all archived data for a specific person, permanently delete it
7. Notifications

Email on job complete/fail
Webhook for enterprise integrations
TECH STACK RATIONALE

Why Cloudflare over AWS:

R2 has zero egress fees. Restoring 100GB costs $0 in transfer. S3 would charge ~$9 per restore. At scale this matters.
Workers have no cold starts. Lambda cold starts add 200–800ms per invocation.
Workflows replace Step Functions — durable, retry-safe, no infrastructure management.
D1 (SQLite) is relational, fast, co-located with Workers. No VPC, no connection pooling headaches.
Everything on one platform, one bill, one API key.
Full Stack:

Next.js (OpenNext → Cloudflare Pages)
├── / (marketing website)
├── /signup, /login (email/password + Google OAuth via NextAuth/Lucia)
├── /dashboard (all orgs, storage health overview)
├── /org/[id] (single org workspace)
│   ├── /overview (storage chart, savings to date, active jobs)
│   ├── /archive (filter → preview → trigger)
│   ├── /jobs (job history, real-time progress)
│   ├── /restore (search archived records, view, restore)
│   ├── /schedule (auto-archive rules)
│   └── /audit (full audit log, export)
└── /admin (super admin: all tenants, org requests, billing overview)

Hono (Cloudflare Worker) — API layer
├── Auth middleware: validates platform JWT, scopes every request to tenant_id
├── /api/auth/* — platform login (NextAuth or Lucia)
├── /api/sf/connect — initiate SF OAuth Web Server flow
├── /api/sf/callback — exchange code → store encrypted tokens in D1
├── /api/sf/setup — Metadata API → deploy custom fields to org
├── /api/storage — storage breakdown (REST API + D1 cache)
├── /api/jobs — CRUD, status polling
├── /api/jobs/:id/preview — COUNT query to SF (returns estimate)
├── /api/view/:record_id — generate presigned R2 URL or render HTML
├── /api/restore — trigger RestoreWorkflow
├── /api/schedule — CRUD for auto-archive rules
├── /api/audit — paginated audit log
└── /api/admin/* — super admin (you only, role-gated)

Cloudflare Queue (vault-jobs)
└── Decouples job trigger from processing
    LWC/dashboard fires POST /api/jobs → job written to D1 → pushed to Queue → returns job_id immediately

Cloudflare Workflows
├── ArchiveWorkflow (one per job, durable, resumable)
└── RestoreWorkflow

Cloudflare Cron Triggers
├── Every hour: process pending scheduled jobs
├── Every hour: report billing_events to Stripe metered usage
└── Every hour: refresh nearly-expired SF tokens

Cloudflare R2
└── vault-data bucket
    /{tenant_id}/{Case|Opportunity}/{record_id}/
      /emails/{email_id}.json
      /attachments/{cv_id}
      /attachments/{cv_id}.meta.json
      /feed/feed_items.json
      /feed/feed_comments.json
      /snapshot.json (Case/Opportunity fields at archive time)

Cloudflare D1 + Drizzle ORM
└── Full schema (below)

Salesforce Org (client side)
Phase 1: Custom fields only, deployed via Metadata API automatically on connect
  Case.vault_Is_Archived__c (Checkbox)
  Case.vault_Archived_At__c (DateTime)

D1 SCHEMA

sql
-- Platform users (your app users, not SF users)
CREATE TABLE users (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  email TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,
  name TEXT,
  password_hash TEXT,           -- null if Google OAuth
  google_id TEXT UNIQUE,
  role TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'user',  -- 'superadmin' | 'user'
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);

-- Billing entity: one company = one tenant
-- One tenant can have multiple SF orgs (prod, sandbox, staging)
CREATE TABLE tenants (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  slug TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,          -- globally unique, URL-safe name
  display_name TEXT NOT NULL,
  owner_user_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id),
  stripe_customer_id TEXT,
  stripe_subscription_id TEXT,
  plan TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'starter',
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);

-- Users invited to a tenant
CREATE TABLE tenant_members (
  tenant_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES tenants(id),
  user_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id),
  role TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'member',  -- 'owner' | 'admin' | 'member'
  invited_by TEXT REFERENCES users(id),
  joined_at INTEGER NOT NULL,
  PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, user_id)
);

-- New tenant requests awaiting super admin approval
CREATE TABLE org_requests (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  tenant_id TEXT,                    -- null until approved
  requested_by TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id),
  slug_requested TEXT NOT NULL,
  sf_type TEXT NOT NULL,             -- 'production' | 'sandbox'
  answers_json TEXT,                 -- onboarding question answers
  status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'pending',  -- pending|approved|rejected
  reviewed_by TEXT REFERENCES users(id),
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL,
  reviewed_at INTEGER
);

-- Connected Salesforce orgs
CREATE TABLE sf_orgs (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  tenant_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES tenants(id),
  sf_org_id TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,    -- 18-char SF Org ID
  sf_instance_url TEXT NOT NULL,
  sf_type TEXT NOT NULL,             -- 'production' | 'sandbox'
  label TEXT NOT NULL,               -- human name: "Production", "Dev Sandbox"
  setup_complete INTEGER DEFAULT 0,  -- 1 = custom fields deployed
  connected_by TEXT REFERENCES users(id),
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);

-- SF OAuth tokens per org (AES-256 encrypted at rest)
CREATE TABLE sf_tokens (
  org_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES sf_orgs(id),
  access_token_enc TEXT NOT NULL,    -- encrypted
  refresh_token_enc TEXT NOT NULL,   -- encrypted
  sf_user_id TEXT NOT NULL,          -- SF user who connected
  expires_at INTEGER NOT NULL,
  updated_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);

-- Archive/Restore jobs
CREATE TABLE jobs (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  org_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES sf_orgs(id),
  tenant_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES tenants(id),
  type TEXT NOT NULL,                    -- 'archive' | 'restore'
  sf_object_type TEXT NOT NULL,          -- 'Case' | 'Opportunity'
  filter_json TEXT NOT NULL,             -- {status, dateRange, recordType, ids[]}
  status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'queued', -- queued|running|done|failed|cancelled
  total_records INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
  processed_records INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
  failed_records INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
  triggered_by_user_id TEXT REFERENCES users(id),
  triggered_by_sf_user_id TEXT,          -- if triggered from LWC
  cf_workflow_id TEXT,                   -- Cloudflare Workflow execution ID
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL,
  started_at INTEGER,
  completed_at INTEGER
);

-- One row per parent record (Case or Opportunity)
-- This is your idempotency layer and progress tracker
CREATE TABLE job_records (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  job_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES jobs(id),
  sf_record_id TEXT NOT NULL,
  status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'pending',  -- pending|processing|archived|restored|failed
  emails_archived INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
  attachments_archived INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
  feed_items_archived INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
  storage_freed_bytes INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
  r2_prefix TEXT,                    -- /{tenant}/{Case}/{record_id}/
  checksum TEXT,                     -- integrity hash
  error TEXT,
  attempts INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
  updated_at INTEGER NOT NULL,
  UNIQUE(job_id, sf_record_id)
);

-- Complete audit trail
CREATE TABLE audit_log (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  tenant_id TEXT NOT NULL,
  org_id TEXT NOT NULL,
  job_id TEXT,
  action TEXT NOT NULL,              -- 'archive'|'restore'|'delete'|'view'|'connect'|'setup'
  actor_user_id TEXT,                -- your platform user
  actor_sf_user_id TEXT,             -- SF user (if from LWC)
  record_count INTEGER,
  meta_json TEXT,
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);

-- Stripe metered usage events
CREATE TABLE billing_events (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  tenant_id TEXT NOT NULL,
  org_id TEXT NOT NULL,
  event_type TEXT NOT NULL,          -- 'archive' | 'restore'
  record_count INTEGER NOT NULL,
  stripe_reported INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);

-- Auto-archive schedules
CREATE TABLE schedules (
  id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  org_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES sf_orgs(id),
  tenant_id TEXT NOT NULL,
  sf_object_type TEXT NOT NULL,
  filter_json TEXT NOT NULL,
  cron_expression TEXT NOT NULL,
  enabled INTEGER DEFAULT 1,
  last_run_at INTEGER,
  last_job_id TEXT,
  created_at INTEGER NOT NULL
);

-- Short-TTL cache for SF API responses (storage breakdown, etc.)
CREATE TABLE sf_cache (
  key TEXT PRIMARY KEY,             -- org_id + ':' + cache_type
  data_json TEXT NOT NULL,
  fetched_at INTEGER NOT NULL,
  ttl_seconds INTEGER NOT NULL
);

CREATE INDEX idx_jobs_org ON jobs(org_id, status);
CREATE INDEX idx_job_records_job ON job_records(job_id, status);
CREATE INDEX idx_audit_tenant ON audit_log(tenant_id, created_at);
CREATE INDEX idx_billing_unreported ON billing_events(stripe_reported, created_at);
CREATE INDEX idx_schedules_org ON schedules(org_id, enabled);
ARCHIVE WORKFLOW — COMPLETE FLOW

TRIGGER:
  User clicks "Start Archive" in dashboard
  POST /api/jobs { org_id, sf_object_type: 'Case', filters: { status: 'Closed', before: '2023-01-01' } }
  
  Hono:
    1. Validate session → get tenant_id
    2. Write job to D1: status='queued'
    3. Push to Cloudflare Queue: { job_id }
    4. Return { job_id } immediately to frontend
  
  Frontend: starts polling GET /api/jobs/:id every 5s → shows progress bar

═══════════════════════════════════════════
ARCHIVE WORKFLOW (Durable, Resumable)
═══════════════════════════════════════════

Step 1 — CLAIM JOB (idempotent)
  Read job from D1
  If status != 'queued' → exit (already running or done)
  Set status = 'running', started_at = now

Step 2 — GET SF ACCESS TOKEN
  Read sf_tokens from D1
  If expires_at < now + 5min → Client Credentials flow → get fresh token → update D1
  Decrypt stored tokens using AES-256 key (stored in Cloudflare secrets)

Step 3 — RESOLVE MATCHING RECORDS
  If filters.ids provided:
    Use those directly
  Else if record count < 2000:
    REST API: SELECT Id FROM Case WHERE {filters} (synchronous, fast)
  Else:
    Bulk API 2.0 Query Job: SELECT Id FROM Case WHERE {filters}
    Poll until complete → read CSV result chunks → collect all IDs
  
  Write each Case ID to D1 job_records: status='pending' (INSERT OR IGNORE — idempotent)
  Update D1 jobs.total_records = count

Step 4 — MARK STATUS IN SF (async, non-blocking)
  Bulk API 2.0 Update: set vault_Archival_Status__c = 'Pending' on all matched IDs
  This shows the admin in SF that archival is in progress

Step 5 — PROCESS IN CHUNKS (resumable)
  Query D1: SELECT * FROM job_records WHERE job_id = ? AND status = 'pending' LIMIT 20
  
  For each chunk of 20 Case IDs:
  
  5a. FETCH ALL RELATED DATA (one Composite API call = 1 API unit)
      5 subrequests in one HTTP call:
      - GET Cases metadata (Id, CaseNumber, Subject, Status, OwnerId)
      - GET EmailMessages WHERE ParentId IN (case_ids) — full fields for archival
      - GET ContentDocumentLinks WHERE LinkedEntityId IN (case_ids)
      - GET FeedItems WHERE ParentId IN (case_ids)
      - GET FeedComments WHERE ParentId IN (case_ids)
  
  5b. FETCH CONTENT VERSIONS
      For each ContentDocumentId found → GET ContentDocument → get LatestPublishedVersionId
      This gives us the actual file ID to stream
  
  5c. WRITE TO R2 (parallel streaming, no memory buffer)
      For each EmailMessage:
        PUT /{tenant_id}/Case/{case_id}/emails/{email_id}.json
        = full EmailMessage JSON (all fields needed for restore)
        Key is unique per email → PUT is idempotent
      
      For each ContentVersion:
        Stream binary from SF API directly to R2 (chunked, no Lambda memory issues)
        PUT /{tenant_id}/Case/{case_id}/attachments/{cv_id}
        PUT /{tenant_id}/Case/{case_id}/attachments/{cv_id}.meta.json
      
      For FeedItems + FeedComments:
        PUT /{tenant_id}/Case/{case_id}/feed/feed_items.json
        PUT /{tenant_id}/Case/{case_id}/feed/feed_comments.json
      
      Write snapshot:
        PUT /{tenant_id}/Case/{case_id}/snapshot.json
        = Case fields (CaseNumber, Subject, Status, etc.) at archive time
  
  5d. COMPUTE + STORE CHECKSUM
      SHA-256 of all archived object IDs for this Case
      Write to D1 job_records.checksum
  
  5e. VERIFY BEFORE DELETE
      Count emails/attachments written to R2 == count fetched from SF for this chunk
      If mismatch → mark those records failed in D1, continue to next chunk
      DO NOT delete from SF if verification fails
  
  5f. DELETE FROM SF (only if 5e passes)
      Composite API, batch of 25:
      - DELETE EmailMessages
      - DELETE ContentVersions (this frees file storage)
      
      Note: deleting ContentVersion → SF also handles ContentDocument/Link cleanup
      
      FeedItems/FeedComments: keep in SF (they're tiny, not worth the restore complexity)
  
  5g. UPDATE SF FIELDS
      Composite PATCH on Cases in this chunk:
      - vault_Is_Archived__c = true
      - vault_Archived_At__c = timestamp
      - vault_Archive_Job_Id__c = job_id
      - vault_Archival_Status__c = 'Archived'
  
  5h. UPDATE D1
      UPDATE job_records SET status='archived', r2_prefix=..., emails_archived=X, 
             attachments_archived=Y, storage_freed_bytes=Z, updated_at=now
             WHERE id = ?
      UPDATE jobs SET processed_records = processed_records + 20 WHERE id = ?
      
      This is what the frontend's progress bar reads
  
  5i. REPEAT from 5a for next chunk
      If workflow crashes here → restart reads D1 → skips already 'archived' records
      This is the core idempotency guarantee

Step 6 — FINALIZE
  Count failed_records from D1
  Update D1 jobs: status='done', completed_at=now, failed_records=count
  
  If failed_records > 0:
    Revert vault_Archival_Status__c = 'Failed' on failed Case IDs in SF
  
  Write billing_events row: { record_count: processed_records, event_type: 'archive' }
  
  Write audit_log row
  
  Send email notification: "Job complete. 4,821 Cases archived. 12.4GB freed. 3 failed."
RESTORE WORKFLOW

TRIGGER:
  POST /api/restore { job_id | record_ids[], org_id }

Step 1 — GET RECORDS TO RESTORE
  If specific record_ids → read job_records from D1
  If full job → read all job_records WHERE job_id = ? AND status = 'archived'

Step 2 — FOR EACH CASE (chunked, resumable)
  2a. Read snapshot.json from R2 → verify case still exists in SF
  2b. Read all emails/{email_id}.json from R2
  2c. Read all attachments/*.meta.json from R2 to get file metadata

Step 3 — RESTORE EMAIL MESSAGES
  Bulk API 2.0 Insert (async, handles any volume):
  Payload includes ALL original fields:
  - Id (Salesforce accepts original ID on insert if org has "Set Audit Fields" enabled)
  - MessageDate, CreatedDate, CreatedById (preserved via audit fields)
  - FromAddress, ToAddress, Subject, HtmlBody, TextBody, Headers
  - ParentId (link back to original Case)
  - ThreadIdentifier (restores email threading)
  
  Note: client must have enabled "Set Audit Fields upon Record Creation" 
  in Setup → User Management Settings (guided in onboarding)

Step 4 — RESTORE CONTENT VERSIONS (FILES)
  For each attachment:
    Stream binary from R2 → POST to SF as new ContentVersion
    POST ContentDocumentLink to link back to Case
  
  Standard permissions, no special flags needed

Step 5 — UPDATE SF + D1
  PATCH Cases: vault_Is_Archived__c = false, vault_Archival_Status__c = null
  UPDATE D1 job_records: status = 'restored'
  Write audit_log row
  Write billing_events row (restore counted separately)
  
Step 6 — OPTIONALLY DELETE FROM R2
  Configurable per-tenant: keep in R2 forever (default) OR delete after restore
VIEWING ARCHIVED CONTENT

Phase 1 (no SF deployment):

GET /api/view/:case_id
  
  Hono:
    1. Validate platform session (user must be logged into your app)
    2. Read all email JSONs from R2 under /{tenant}/Case/{case_id}/emails/
    3. Read attachment metadata JSONs
    4. Build HTML page:
       - Email thread sorted by MessageDate
       - Each email: From, To, Date, Subject, HtmlBody rendered in sandboxed iframe
       - Attachments section: download links (30-second presigned R2 URLs)
    5. Return rendered HTML
  
  Frontend: shows in a modal/drawer inside your dashboard
Phase 2 (LWC in Salesforce):

LWC on Case page detects vault_Is_Archived__c = true
LWC → GET /api/view/:case_id (your Hono API)
LWC renders response in <iframe srcdoc="..."> sandboxed — looks native
Restore button: POST /api/restore from LWC context
ONBOARDING FLOW (STEP BY STEP)

1. Admin hits vault.com → reads marketing page → clicks "Get Started"

2. Signs up with email + password OR Google OAuth
   Creates their platform account (users table)

3. "Request Organization" form:
   - Organization name (globally unique slug, e.g. "acme-corp")
   - Salesforce type: Production / Sandbox
   - Approx record volume: <10k / 10k-100k / 100k-1M / 1M+
   - Primary use case: Case emails / Opportunity attachments / Both
   → Saved to org_requests table, status='pending'
   → You (super admin) get email notification

4. Super admin reviews at vault.com/admin
   → Clicks "Approve" → tenant + sf_org rows created → user notified

5. User sees onboarding checklist:
   □ Step 1: Connect your Salesforce org
   □ Step 2: Enable "Set Audit Fields upon Record Creation" in SF
   □ Step 3: Install our Connected App in your SF org
   □ Step 4: Run storage analysis

6. Step 1 — Connect SF:
   User clicks "Connect Salesforce"
   → Redirect to SF login (OAuth Web Server Flow + PKCE)
   → SF login page → admin logs in → "Allow" screen
   → Redirect to vault.com/api/sf/callback?code=...
   → Hono exchanges code for access_token + refresh_token
   → Store encrypted in D1 sf_tokens
   → sf_orgs.setup_complete = 0 still

7. Step 2 — Guide admin to enable audit fields:
   Show screenshot/video: Setup → User Management Settings → 
   "Enable Set Audit Fields upon Record Creation" → Save
   Then: create Permission Set, add "Set Audit Fields" permission,
   assign to the Connected App's running user
   
   User clicks "I've done this" → we do a quick API test to verify

8. Step 3 — Install Connected App:
   Per September 2025 SF security changes, admin must formally install our app
   Show: Setup → Connected Apps OAuth Usage → find "Vault" → click "Install"
   User clicks "Done" → Hono calls Metadata API:
   - Deploy custom fields to Case and Opportunity objects
   - sf_orgs.setup_complete = 1

9. Step 4 — Storage Analysis runs automatically:
   GET /api/storage → Hono calls SF REST API
   Shows: storage breakdown, estimated overage cost, projected savings
   User sees the money → they're ready to archive

Total onboarding time target: under 15 minutes
SALESFORCE SIDE: WHAT WE DEPLOY (METADATA API)

No managed package needed for Phase 1. When setup runs, Hono calls SF Metadata API and deploys:

Custom Fields on Case:
  vault__Is_Archived__c          Checkbox, default false
  vault__Archived_At__c          DateTime
  vault__Archive_Job_Id__c       Text(18)
  vault__Archival_Status__c      Picklist: Pending, Archived, Failed

Custom Fields on Opportunity:
  (same four fields)

Custom Object: vault__Archival_Progress__c
  vault__Status__c               Picklist: Running, Completed, Failed
  vault__Total_Records__c        Number
  vault__Processed_Records__c    Number
  vault__Failed_Records__c       Number
  vault__Job_Id__c               Text(36)  -- matches D1 job.id
Everything deployed programmatically. Client runs zero Salesforce setup except the two human steps (audit fields setting + Connected App install — both guided with screenshots).

Growth:      $149/month — up to 3 orgs, 500k records/month
Enterprise:  $499/month — unlimited orgs, BYOC, SLA, webhook, audit export
Overage:     $0.002/record above plan limit

  At ~100k EmailMessages = 600MB–1GB of data storage used by emails alone
  Vault Starter at $49: saves them $76/month immediately, net positive day 1
  
  Client paying $2,500/month overage (10GB emails + 40GB attachments):
  Vault Growth at $149: saves them $2,351/month. 15x ROI.
WHAT YOU'RE NOT BUILDING (PHASE 1)

LWC managed package (Phase 2, after first 3 paying clients)
BYOC (bring your own R2/S3) — Enterprise tier only, Phase 3
AppExchange listing — after PMF, skip for first 10 clients
Sandbox seeding (different product, different buyer)
Full backup/recovery (OwnBackup already owns that, don't compete)
BUILD ORDER

Week 1-2:
  - Next.js marketing page + auth (email/pass + Google)
  - Org request form + super admin approval flow
  - SF OAuth Web Server flow (connect → callback → store tokens)

Week 3-4:
  - Metadata API: auto-deploy custom fields on org connect
  - Storage analysis endpoint (REST API + D1 cache)
  - Storage breakdown UI with cost calculation

Week 5-6:
  - ArchiveWorkflow skeleton: fetch + write to R2 only (no delete yet)
  - D1 job_records tracking
  - Frontend progress bar (polling /api/jobs/:id)

Week 7:
  - Delete step in workflow (EmailMessage + ContentVersion)
  - SF field update step (vault_Is_Archived__c, vault_Archival_Status__c)

Week 8:
  - Archive viewer: HTML render from R2, modal in dashboard
  - Billing events → Stripe metered usage integration

Week 9:
  - Restore workflow
  - Email notifications on job complete/fail

Week 10+:
  - Scheduled auto-archive
  - GDPR erasure
  - LWC managed package (Phase 2)

Claude responded: Good question.
Good question. Let me think through this carefully — what does the user actually need to see and do on each page.

THE USER'S MENTAL MODEL

They think: "I have 50,000 old closed Cases sitting in Salesforce eating storage. I want to archive them. I want to see what happened. I want to find one and restore it if needed."

They do NOT want to select individual records row by row. That's 50,000 clicks. They want to define a filter and say "archive everything matching this."

PAGE BY PAGE — WHAT'S ON SCREEN

SUMMARY: WHEN DO YOU SHOW A RECORD TABLE

Only on the Restore/View page. That table comes from D1, not SF. It's fast, paginated, searchable.

Everywhere else — archive, overview, jobs — it's aggregate views, filters, and progress bars. The user never selects individual records for archiving. They define rules, preview the count, confirm. The system handles the rest.

Searched the web
Searched the web
Good. Now I have the full picture. Let me answer both questions directly.

QUESTION 1: WHAT DO WE ACTUALLY STORE IN D1?

Short answer: D1 stores metadata and state. Never the actual data.

Here's the exact split:

D1 stores:

Job state (status, progress counts, who triggered it, when)
One row per archived Case/Opportunity (the job_records table) — just IDs, status, r2_prefix, checksum, error. Not the actual emails.
Audit log entries
SF OAuth tokens (encrypted)
SF data cache (storage breakdown numbers, TTL'd)
Billing events
R2 stores:

Actual email JSON (HtmlBody, TextBody, all fields)
Attachment binaries
Feed data
SF still stores:

The Case/Opportunity record itself (tiny, 2KB)
Your custom fields (vault__Is_Archived__c etc.)
So the restore page table you saw earlier — it queries D1 job_records to show which Cases are archived. Fast, no SF or R2 API calls. When user clicks View, THEN you go to R2 to fetch the actual email content.

D1 row for a job_record is roughly 200 bytes. 1 million archived Cases = ~200MB in D1. Fine.

QUESTION 2: SHOWING ARCHIVED CONTENT ON THE SALESFORCE CASE PAGE

The honest answer has three options with very different trade-offs.

Option A — Visualforce Page (deployable via Metadata API, no managed package)

This is the key insight: you can deploy a Visualforce page to a client's org programmatically using the Metadata API, just like you deploy custom fields. No AppExchange, no package install URL, no manual steps beyond the one-time setup your onboarding already does.

What it looks like: a section appears on the Case detail page called "Archived Emails & Attachments". When the Case is archived (vault__Is_Archived__c = true), it shows the content fetched from your API. When not archived, the section is hidden or shows nothing.

How it works technically:

During org setup (already happens for custom fields),
your Hono backend also deploys via Metadata API:

1. A Visualforce page: VaultViewer
   - Receives Case ID from the page context
   - Makes a callout to your Hono API: GET /view/{case_id}
   - Renders the HTML response in an iframe (srcdoc)
   - Shows attachment list with download buttons
   - Shows "Restore to Salesforce" button

2. Page Layout update (also Metadata API):
   - Adds VaultViewer as a section on Case page layout
   - Height: 400px, width: 100%
   - Visibility: conditional on vault__Is_Archived__c

3. CSP Trusted Site entry:
   - Adds your domain (vault.com) to SF's trusted sites
   - Required for the VF page to call your API
The Visualforce page code itself is simple:

html
<apex:page standardController="Case" showHeader="false" sidebar="false">
  <apex:outputPanel rendered="{!Case.vault__Is_Archived__c}">
    <div style="padding: 8px;">
      <iframe
        srcdoc="{!vaultContent}"
        width="100%"
        height="500px"
        frameborder="0"
        sandbox="allow-scripts allow-same-origin allow-popups">
      </iframe>
    </div>
  </apex:outputPanel>
  <apex:outputPanel rendered="{!!Case.vault__Is_Archived__c}">
    <p style="color:#666; padding:8px;">
      No archived content for this case.
    </p>
  </apex:outputPanel>
</apex:page>
The Apex controller behind it calls your Hono API using an HTTP callout, gets the rendered HTML, passes it to srcdoc. Renders inline, looks native, no external tab needed.

But wait — this requires Apex. Apex requires Apex test classes with 75%+ code coverage to deploy to production. That's real overhead. Not impossible, but it's not as simple as deploying a custom field.

Option B — Custom Button + External URL (zero Apex, zero complexity)

The simplest possible SF-side integration. No Visualforce, no Apex, no component.

During setup, Metadata API deploys one thing: a custom button on Case called "View Archived Emails".

Button type: URL. Target URL:

https://vault.com/sf-view/{!Case.Id}?token={!$Api.Session_ID}
User clicks button → new tab opens → shows your Next.js page with the archived emails for that Case rendered beautifully. You validate the SF Session ID against the connected org to authenticate the user.

Pros: zero Apex, deploys in seconds via Metadata API, works today, secure.
Cons: opens a new tab, not inline on the page.

For Phase 1, this is the right call. Fast to ship, zero complexity.

Option C — Full LWC (Phase 2)

Proper LWC component deployed via Metadata API. Inline on the Case page. Shows archived content in a drawer/panel without leaving Salesforce. Requires LWC code + Apex controller + test classes. 2–3 weeks of extra work. Right for Phase 2 after you have paying clients.

RECOMMENDED APPROACH BY PHASE

Phase 1 (ship fast):
  Deployed via Metadata API during onboarding setup:
  ├── 4 custom fields on Case (vault__Is_Archived__c etc.)
  ├── 4 custom fields on Opportunity
  ├── Custom Button: "View Archived Emails" → your URL
  ├── Page layout update: add button to Case layout
  └── CSP Trusted Site: vault.com

  User experience:
  Case is archived → custom field shows "Archived"
  Agent sees "View Archived Emails" button on Case
  Clicks → opens vault.com/sf-view/[case_id] in new tab
  Sees full email thread + attachment downloads
  "Restore to Salesforce" button on that page
  
  No Apex. No test classes. No complexity.
  Deploys in < 30 seconds via Metadata API.

Phase 2 (after 3+ paying clients):
  Add to Metadata API deployment:
  ├── Apex class: VaultViewerController (callout to your API)
  ├── Apex test class (75% coverage requirement)
  ├── Visualforce page: VaultViewer (inline iframe)
  └── Page layout update: add VF section, remove button

  User experience:
  Section "Archived Emails" appears on Case page
  When archived: shows email thread inline, no new tab
  Attachment downloads, restore button — all inline
THE AUTHENTICATION QUESTION FOR THE BUTTON FLOW

When user clicks the button and lands on your page at vault.com/sf-view/[case_id]?token=[session_id]:

Your Hono backend validates the session token by calling SF's identity endpoint:

GET {instance_url}/services/oauth2/userinfo
Authorization: Bearer {session_token}
If valid → SF returns the user's org ID and user ID. You match org ID against your D1 sf_orgs table → confirm this user's org has this case archived → serve the content.

If invalid → 401, show login page.

This is secure. The SF session token is short-lived (hours) and org-scoped. You never store it.

SUMMARY

D1 stores job state and record metadata only. R2 stores actual content. SF stores the Case shell + custom fields.

For showing archived content on the Case page: Phase 1 is a custom button deployed via Metadata API — opens your page in a new tab, zero Apex required. Phase 2 is a Visualforce page with inline iframe — requires Apex but stays fully within Salesforce. Phase 3 is a full LWC component.

All three deployed programmatically via Metadata API during your existing onboarding setup step. The client does nothing manually except approve the deployment.

Good. Now I have the exact limits. Let me answer both questions fully.

QUESTION 1: CUSTOM FIELDS AND OBJECTS — WHAT, WHERE, HOW

What needs to go on the Salesforce side:

Custom fields are deployed programmatically via Metadata API during your onboarding setup step. The client does nothing manually.

Custom Fields on Case object:
  vault__Is_Archived__c          Checkbox, default false
  vault__Archival_Status__c      Picklist: Pending | Archived | Failed
  vault__Archived_At__c          DateTime
  vault__Archive_Job_Id__c       Text(36) — your D1 job UUID

Custom Fields on Opportunity (same four fields):
  vault__Is_Archived__c
  vault__Archival_Status__c
  vault__Archived_At__c
  vault__Archive_Job_Id__c

Custom Object: vault__Archival_Progress__c
  vault__Status__c               Picklist: Running | Completed | Failed
  vault__Total_Records__c        Number
  vault__Processed_Records__c    Number
  vault__Failed_Records__c       Number
  vault__Job_Id__c               Text(36)
  vault__Started_At__c           DateTime
  vault__Completed_At__c         DateTime
Phase 1 also deploys via Metadata API:

CSP Trusted Site entry for your domain
Custom Button on Case: "View Archived Content" pointing to your URL
Page layout update: adds the button to Case layout
Phase 2 also deploys:

Visualforce page (VaultViewer)
Apex controller class + test class
Updated page layout: replaces button with VF section
How deployment works from your Hono backend:

Metadata API uses a zip-based deploy pattern. You POST a base64-encoded zip containing the metadata XML files. Hono generates this zip on the fly from templates, POSTs to /services/data/v66.0/metadata/deployRequest, then polls the deploy job until complete. Takes about 5–15 seconds for this small payload.

The vault__Archival_Progress__c custom object is optional — you can drop it entirely if you track all job progress in D1 only. Simpler. Your D1 jobs table already has total_records, processed_records, failed_records, status. That's your source of truth. The SF object was only needed in the old architecture because you had no external DB. You have D1 now. Drop the SF custom object. Keep only the 4 custom fields on Case and Opportunity.

QUESTION 2: CLOUDFLARE WORKERS/WORKFLOWS — LIMITS AND API APPROACH

Exact limits you need to know:

Each Worker isolate has 128MB memory. CPU time limit is 30 seconds by default, configurable up to 5 minutes on Paid plan. Waiting on network requests does NOT count toward CPU time — only actual code execution does. 
Cloudflare

A Workflow instance supports 10,000 steps by default, configurable to 25,000. Each step can persist up to 1MB of return value. Total persisted state per instance: 1GB on Paid plan. A Workflow instance can run forever as long as each step stays within CPU limits. 
Cloudflare

10,000 concurrent Workflow instances. 100 new instances created per second. 
Cloudflare

The key insight about CPU time vs wall time:

CPU time = time your code is actually computing (JSON.parse, SHA256, string manipulation, etc.). Wall time = total elapsed time including waiting for SF API responses, R2 writes, D1 queries.

A step that makes 5 SF API calls each taking 2 seconds = 10 seconds wall time, but maybe 50ms CPU time. You will never hit CPU limits doing I/O-heavy work. The 128MB memory limit is the real constraint.

THE PROBLEM WITH YOUR CURRENT APPROACH (AND WHY IT EXISTS)

Your current architecture: 10k records → split into batches of 20 → 500 Lambdas in parallel via Step Functions.

Why you did this: Lambda has a 15-minute execution limit and 10GB memory max per invocation. You needed parallelism to process large volumes without hitting the timeout. Each Lambda handles 20 records, does REST/Composite API calls, streams to S3, updates SF status.

The problems: race conditions on the flat JSON files (multiple Lambdas writing to the same email.json), no idempotency if a Lambda crashes mid-way, progress tracking in SF instead of your own DB, username/password auth.

The Cloudflare approach is fundamentally different because Workflows has durable execution. You don't need 500 parallel workers. You have one Workflow instance that runs in steps, checkpoints after each step, can hibernate for hours, and resumes exactly where it left off if it crashes. This eliminates the entire class of race condition and glitch problems you have now.

WHICH SF API TO USE WHEN

Three different tools for three different jobs:

Bulk API 2.0 — for anything over ~2,000 records. Async. SF processes in background. You submit a job, poll for completion, read results as CSV chunks. No timeout risk. Does NOT count against your daily REST API quota (query jobs). The right tool for: fetching 50,000 Case IDs matching a filter, bulk-updating vault__Is_Archived__c on 50,000 records, bulk-deleting 50,000 EmailMessages.

Composite REST API — for anything under ~2,000 records or when you need multiple related objects together in one call. Up to 25 subrequests per call, counts as 1 API unit. The right tool for: fetching EmailMessages + ContentDocumentLinks + FeedItems for a batch of 20 Cases in a single HTTP call.

Plain REST API — for single record operations, token refresh, storage analysis queries, status updates on small batches.

THE FULL PROCESSING ARCHITECTURE

The key architectural decision: one Workflow per job, not one Workflow per batch.

One Workflow handles the entire job for a tenant's archive request. It uses steps to chunk through records. No parallel Lambdas. Sequential but fast because most time is spent waiting on I/O (which doesn't count as CPU time).

Here is exactly how it works:

Job triggered: archive 50,000 Cases for tenant X

WORKFLOW INSTANCE: ArchiveWorkflow-{job_id}

─── STEP 1: CLAIM JOB ─────────────────────────────
  Read job from D1
  If status != 'queued' → exit (idempotent)
  Update D1: status = 'running', started_at = now
  Returns: { job_id, org_id, tenant_id, filter_json }

─── STEP 2: RESOLVE SF TOKEN ──────────────────────
  Read sf_tokens from D1
  Decrypt access_token (AES-256 using CF secret)
  If expires_at < now + 10min:
    Client Credentials flow → fresh access_token
    Encrypt + update D1
  Returns: { access_token, instance_url }

─── STEP 3: GET MATCHING RECORD IDs ───────────────
  Record count check first (1 REST call):
    SELECT COUNT() FROM Case WHERE {filters}
  
  If count <= 2000:
    REST: SELECT Id FROM Case WHERE {filters}
    → all IDs in one synchronous response
  
  If count > 2000:
    Bulk API 2.0 Query Job:
      POST /jobs/query { object: 'Case', query: 'SELECT Id FROM Case WHERE...' }
      Returns job_id immediately
      
      Poll loop (step.sleep between polls):
        GET /jobs/query/{bulk_job_id}
        If state != 'JobComplete' → step.sleep('poll', '10 seconds') → retry
      
      GET /jobs/query/{bulk_job_id}/results
      → SF returns CSV stream
      Parse CSV chunks → collect all Case IDs
  
  Batch IDs into chunks of 20
  Write each ID to D1 job_records: 
    INSERT OR IGNORE (sf_record_id, job_id, status='pending')
    ← INSERT OR IGNORE is the idempotency guarantee
  Update D1 jobs.total_records = count
  Returns: { total_count, chunk_count }

─── STEP 4: MARK SF STATUS = PENDING ──────────────
  Bulk API 2.0 Ingest Job (Update):
    Object: Case
    Fields: vault__Archival_Status__c = 'Pending'
    Records: all IDs from step 3
  This shows progress in SF without touching data
  Fire-and-forget, non-blocking for the main flow

─── STEPS 5..N: PROCESS CHUNKS (resumable loop) ───
  
  Query D1: 
    SELECT sf_record_id FROM job_records 
    WHERE job_id = ? AND status = 'pending' 
    LIMIT 20
  
  If no pending records → jump to finalize step
  
  For each chunk of 20 Case IDs:
  
  ┌─── STEP 5a: FETCH RELATED DATA ──────────────────
  │  One Composite API call (counts as 1 API unit):
  │  POST /composite with 5 subrequests:
  │    [0] GET Cases: SELECT Id,CaseNumber,Subject,Status,
  │                   OwnerId,CreatedDate FROM Case 
  │                   WHERE Id IN ('id1','id2',...'id20')
  │    [1] GET EmailMessages: SELECT ALL_FIELDS FROM EmailMessage 
  │                           WHERE ParentId IN (...) 
  │    [2] GET ContentDocumentLinks: SELECT Id,ContentDocumentId,
  │                                  LinkedEntityId 
  │                                  FROM ContentDocumentLink 
  │                                  WHERE LinkedEntityId IN (...)
  │    [3] GET FeedItems: SELECT ALL_FIELDS FROM FeedItem 
  │                       WHERE ParentId IN (...)
  │    [4] GET FeedComments: SELECT ALL_FIELDS FROM FeedComment 
  │                          WHERE ParentId IN (...)
  │
  │  Returns: all related data for 20 Cases in one HTTP call
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ┌─── STEP 5b: FETCH CONTENT VERSIONS ──────────────
  │  From step 5a we have ContentDocumentIds
  │  Batch ContentDocument query (one REST call):
  │    SELECT Id,LatestPublishedVersionId,Title,FileExtension
  │    FROM ContentDocument 
  │    WHERE Id IN ('docid1','docid2',...)
  │  Returns: file metadata including version IDs
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ┌─── STEP 5c: WRITE EMAILS TO R2 ──────────────────
  │  For each EmailMessage in this chunk:
  │    key = /{tenant_id}/Case/{case_id}/emails/{email_id}.json
  │    value = JSON.stringify(full EmailMessage object)
  │    
  │    R2 PUT is idempotent — safe to retry
  │    No memory issue: JSON is text, 10-50KB per email
  │    20 Cases × avg 5 emails = 100 R2 PUTs
  │    These happen sequentially or with Promise.all(max 10)
  │
  │  For each FeedItem/FeedComment:
  │    key = /{tenant_id}/Case/{case_id}/feed/items.json
  │    PUT the array (idempotent overwrite)
  │
  │  Write Case snapshot:
  │    key = /{tenant_id}/Case/{case_id}/snapshot.json
  │    value = Case metadata (CaseNumber, Subject, Status, etc.)
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ┌─── STEP 5d: STREAM ATTACHMENTS TO R2 ────────────
  │  THIS IS THE MEMORY-SENSITIVE PART
  │
  │  For each ContentVersion in this chunk:
  │    SF attachment URL: 
  │      {instance_url}/services/data/v66.0/sobjects/
  │      ContentVersion/{version_id}/VersionData
  │    
  │    KEY PATTERN (avoids memory issue):
  │    const sfResponse = await fetch(sfUrl, { 
  │      headers: { Authorization: `Bearer ${token}` } 
  │    })
  │    // sfResponse.body is a ReadableStream
  │    // R2 accepts ReadableStream directly
  │    await env.R2.put(r2key, sfResponse.body, {
  │      httpMetadata: { contentType: mimeType }
  │    })
  │    // Stream goes SF → R2 directly
  │    // Zero bytes loaded into Worker memory
  │    // Worker is just a pipe
  │
  │  Write .meta.json alongside each binary:
  │    { filename, mimeType, size, cvId, docId, parentCaseId }
  │
  │  DO NOT Promise.all() all attachments at once
  │  Worker has 128MB memory, a single isolate
  │  may handle multiple concurrent requests
  │  Process max 3 attachments in parallel:
  │    await Promise.all(batch.slice(0,3).map(streamOne))
  │    then next 3, etc.
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ┌─── STEP 5e: VERIFY ──────────────────────────────
  │  Count emails written to R2 for this chunk
  │  vs emails fetched from SF for this chunk
  │  
  │  In practice: check the R2 HEAD requests return 200
  │  Or: track written IDs in step return value (up to 1MB)
  │  
  │  If mismatch → mark these job_records as 'failed' in D1
  │                → continue to next chunk (don't abort job)
  │  If verified → proceed to delete
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ┌─── STEP 5f: DELETE FROM SF ──────────────────────
  │  Only runs if 5e passed for this chunk
  │  
  │  Composite API (25 subrequests max per call):
  │  Split EmailMessage IDs into batches of 25:
  │    DELETE /sobjects/EmailMessage/{id} × 25
  │  
  │  Separate call for ContentVersions:
  │    DELETE /sobjects/ContentDocument/{id} × 25
  │    (deleting ContentDocument cascades to ContentVersion + Links)
  │
  │  If > 25 deletes needed: multiple Composite calls
  │  Each call = 1 API unit regardless of subrequests
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ┌─── STEP 5g: UPDATE SF CASE FIELDS ───────────────
  │  Composite PATCH on the 20 Cases:
  │    vault__Is_Archived__c = true
  │    vault__Archival_Status__c = 'Archived'
  │    vault__Archived_At__c = ISO timestamp
  │    vault__Archive_Job_Id__c = job_id
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ┌─── STEP 5h: UPDATE D1 ───────────────────────────
  │  UPDATE job_records SET 
  │    status = 'archived',
  │    r2_prefix = '/{tenant}/Case/{case_id}/',
  │    emails_archived = X,
  │    attachments_archived = Y,
  │    updated_at = now
  │  WHERE sf_record_id IN (...)
  │  
  │  UPDATE jobs SET
  │    processed_records = processed_records + 20
  │  WHERE id = ?
  │  
  │  ← This is what the frontend progress bar reads
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ─── LOOP: go back, query D1 for next 20 pending records
  ─── Repeat until no pending records remain

─── FINALIZE STEP ──────────────────────────────────
  Count failed_records from D1
  Update D1 jobs: status='done', completed_at=now
  
  If any failed → bulk update SF: vault__Archival_Status__c='Failed'
    on those Case IDs
  
  Write billing_events row (for Stripe metered usage)
  Write audit_log row
  Send email notification
  Done.
WHY THIS IS BETTER THAN 500 PARALLEL LAMBDAS

The parallel Lambda approach trades complexity for speed. Each Lambda runs fast (20 records), but you get race conditions (multiple writers on same file), no resume capability (Lambda crash = lost progress, need to retry from scratch), and complex orchestration logic.

The Workflow approach is sequential per chunk but solves every problem:

If it crashes after step 5f on chunk 47 → on restart it reads D1, sees chunks 1–47 are archived, skips them, resumes from chunk 48. Zero data loss.
No race conditions. One writer per job.
No flat JSON file accumulation. One R2 key per record.
Progress visible in real-time via D1.
Is it slower? For 50,000 Cases: each chunk of 20 takes roughly 3–8 seconds wall time (SF API calls + R2 writes). 2,500 chunks × ~5 seconds = ~3.5 hours for a massive job. But this runs overnight, users don't care. They set it and check tomorrow.

For typical jobs (1,000–5,000 Cases): 50–250 chunks × 5 seconds = 4–21 minutes. Totally acceptable.

If speed matters more than simplicity (e.g., a paying enterprise client with 500k records wants it fast), you can spawn multiple Workflow instances processing different ID ranges in parallel. But that's an optimization for later.

MEMORY SAFETY: THE CRITICAL RULES

The 128MB limit per isolate is the constraint you must design around.

What's safe: fetching JSON (EmailMessage bodies are 10–100KB each), writing JSON to R2, reading from D1, making API calls.

What's dangerous: loading attachment binaries into memory. A single 25MB PDF loaded as arrayBuffer() = 25MB of heap. 20 attachments per chunk = 500MB. Instant OOM.

The solution is already in step 5d: stream directly from SF to R2 using ReadableStream. The Worker never holds the binary in memory. SF serves it, Worker passes the stream handle to R2, R2 pulls from SF. Worker memory usage for a 100MB attachment: ~a few KB for the stream handle.

typescript
// WRONG — loads entire file into Worker memory
const binary = await sfResponse.arrayBuffer()  // 25MB in heap
await env.R2.put(key, binary)

// RIGHT — streams directly, zero memory
const sfResponse = await fetch(sfUrl, { headers: { Authorization: `Bearer ${token}` } })
await env.R2.put(key, sfResponse.body)  // ReadableStream, ~0 bytes in heap
PARALLEL ATTACHMENT STREAMING — THE RIGHT CONCURRENCY

Don't Promise.all() all attachments in a chunk simultaneously. A chunk of 20 Cases might have 60 attachments. 60 open HTTP streams simultaneously = memory pressure + SF API rate limit risk.

Use a concurrency limiter: process 5 at a time.

typescript
const CONCURRENCY = 5
for (let i = 0; i < attachments.length; i += CONCURRENCY) {
  await Promise.all(
    attachments.slice(i, i + CONCURRENCY).map(att => streamToR2(att))
  )
}
COMPLETE LIMITS CHEATSHEET FOR THIS ARCHITECTURE

Cloudflare Workers:
  Memory per isolate:        128 MB (shared across concurrent requests)
  CPU time default:          30 seconds
  CPU time max (Paid):       5 minutes
  Wall time:                 unlimited while client connected
  
  Key rule: SF API fetch() waiting = 0 CPU time
  Your actual CPU per chunk: ~50-100ms (JSON parse, SHA256, D1 queries)
  You will NEVER hit CPU limit doing I/O work

Cloudflare Workflows:
  Max steps per instance:    10,000 default, 25,000 configurable
  Step return value:         1 MB max (don't return large payloads from steps)
  Total persisted state:     1 GB per instance (Paid)
  Concurrent instances:      10,000
  New instances per second:  100
  
  Key rule: sleeping/waiting instances don't count toward concurrency
  Your archive job: ~2,500 steps for 50k Cases (within default limit)
  
  Step count math:
    50,000 Cases / 20 per chunk = 2,500 processing steps
    + setup steps = ~2,510 total
    Well within 10,000 default limit
  
  100,000 Cases / 20 = 5,000 steps — still fine
  250,000 Cases / 20 = 12,500 steps — increase to 25,000 in config

Salesforce API (client's org, shared with all their other integrations):
  Daily REST calls:          100,000 + (1,000 × user licenses)
  Bulk API query jobs:       No daily limit on query jobs
  Bulk API ingest batches:   15,000 per 24 hours (for updates/deletes)
  Composite subrequests:     25 per call, counts as 1 API unit
  Concurrent long requests:  25 in production
  
  Your consumption for 50k Cases:
    Bulk query (get IDs):    ~0 API units (query jobs don't count)
    Composite fetches:       2,500 chunks × 1 call = 2,500 API units
    Delete calls:            ~5,000 units (200 delete batches of 25)
    SF status updates:       ~5,000 units (Bulk ingest counts separately)
    Total REST units:        ~7,500 of client's daily limit
  
  For a 100-user Enterprise org: 200,000 daily limit
  7,500 is 3.75% of their limit — no problem
WHEN TO USE BULK API vs COMPOSITE vs REST — DECISION RULES

Fetching IDs for a filter (COUNT unknown):
  < 2,000 records expected?  → REST SOQL (synchronous, simple)
  > 2,000 records possible?  → Bulk API 2.0 Query Job (async, no limit)

Fetching related data for known IDs (20 at a time):
  Always → Composite API (5 subrequests, 1 API unit, gets everything)

Deleting records:
  < 200 records? → Composite DELETE (batches of 25, multiple calls)
  > 200 records? → Bulk API 2.0 Ingest (delete operation, async)

Updating custom fields on parent records:
  < 200 records? → Composite PATCH (batches of 25)
  > 200 records? → Bulk API 2.0 Update

Streaming attachment binaries:
  Always → Plain REST GET with stream=true → pipe to R2
  Never use Bulk API for file content — it's for structured data
