Good. Now I have exact numbers. Let me answer this clearly.

Your mental model is right but the bottleneck isn't where you think.

Queue consumer Workers have no CPU time limit — only wall time limits, measured in minutes not seconds. Specifically 15 minutes wall time for Queue consumers. 
DEV Community
Cloudflare

So yes, Queue consumer = 15 min wall time, no CPU limit. That's the right primitive for heavy processing work.

But your proposed architecture has a fundamental problem. Let me explain.

THE PROBLEM WITH PARALLEL QUEUE WORKERS HITTING SALESFORCE

You want: 10k records → split into batches of 20 → 500 queue messages → 500 concurrent queue consumers → all firing Composite API calls at SF simultaneously.

Salesforce caps concurrent long-running API requests at 25 in production orgs, and only 5 in developer/sandbox orgs. A request is "long-running" if it takes 20+ seconds. Your Composite calls fetching emails + attachments for 20 cases will frequently exceed 20 seconds. 
Coefficient

So you have 500 queue workers all hammering the same SF org. SF will start returning REQUEST_LIMIT_EXCEEDED on everything past the 25th concurrent call. Your workers retry. More pile up. You've built a thundering herd against a single SF org.

This is the exact same problem your current Step Functions architecture has — 150 parallel Lambdas all hitting the same SF sandbox simultaneously. The "glitchy" behavior you described is this.

Parallel workers are fast against your own infrastructure. They're dangerous against a rate-limited third-party API that your customer shares with their entire company.

THE REAL CONSTRAINTS YOU'RE DESIGNING AROUND

Your infra (Cloudflare):     No meaningful limit on parallelism
SF concurrent long requests: 25 production / 5 sandbox
SF daily REST API calls:     100k + (1k × users) per 24h
SF Bulk ingest batches:      15,000 per 24h (shared)
The bottleneck is always SF, not Cloudflare. Any architecture that ignores this will be glitchy.

THE RIGHT ARCHITECTURE: WORKFLOW + CONTROLLED PARALLELISM

The answer isn't "one sequential Workflow" OR "500 parallel queue workers". It's controlled fan-out with a concurrency cap that respects SF's limits.

Here's exactly how it works:

Job arrives: archive 10,000 Cases for org X

COORDINATOR WORKFLOW (1 instance per job)
│
│  Step 1: Resolve all Case IDs → 10,000 IDs
│           Write all to D1 job_records (INSERT OR IGNORE)
│
│  Step 2: Fan out into batches
│           Split 10,000 IDs into 500 batches of 20
│           Push all 500 messages to vault-batch-queue
│           Each message: { job_id, org_id, tenant_id, case_ids: [...20 ids] }
│
│  Step 3: Sleep and wait
│           step.waitForEvent('job-complete') 
│           OR step.sleep polling D1 every 60 seconds
│           checking: COUNT(*) FROM job_records WHERE status='pending' = 0
│
│  Step 4: Finalize (all batches done)
│           Update SF status fields in bulk
│           Write billing events
│           Send notification

QUEUE: vault-batch-queue
  max_concurrency: 10  ← THIS IS THE KEY
  
  10 workers processing simultaneously
  Each worker handles 1 batch of 20 Cases
  Each batch makes ~3-4 SF API calls (Composite + file fetches)
  = max ~40 concurrent SF connections at any time
  Well within SF's 25 concurrent long-running limit
  (Most calls complete < 20s so don't count toward the concurrent cap)

BATCH WORKER (queue consumer, 15 min wall time)
│
│  Receives: { job_id, org_id, tenant_id, case_ids }
│
│  1. Get SF token from D1 (decrypt)
│  2. Composite API call for these 20 cases (emails, attachments, feed)
│  3. Fetch ContentDocument metadata
│  4. Stream all email JSONs to R2 (parallel, max 5 at once)
│  5. Stream all attachment binaries to R2 (sequential, memory safe)
│  6. Verify R2 writes
│  7. DELETE from SF (Composite, batches of 25)
│  8. UPDATE D1 job_records: status = 'archived'
│  9. message.ack()  ← tells Queue this batch is done
│
│  If anything fails → message.retry() → Queue retries automatically
│  D1 INSERT OR IGNORE on step 1 means retries are idempotent
WHY max_concurrency: 10 AND NOT 500

If you have a workflow that is limited by an upstream API, you may prefer for your backlog to grow, trading off increased latency in order to avoid overwhelming an upstream system. That's exactly your situation. 
Cloudflare

The math:

SF concurrent long-running limit: 25 (production)
Your calls per batch worker:      2-3 calls (Composite + file streams)
Safe concurrent workers:          ~8-10

At 10 concurrent workers:
  10 batches × 20 cases = 200 cases processing simultaneously
  10,000 cases ÷ 200 per wave = 50 waves
  Each wave takes ~5-10 seconds wall time
  50 waves × 7 seconds = ~350 seconds = ~6 minutes for 10k cases
  
At 500 concurrent workers (your original idea):
  SF returns REQUEST_LIMIT_EXCEEDED on ~475 of them
  Workers retry → exponential backoff → total time: hours
  Race conditions on SF status updates
  Glitchy exactly like your current system
10 concurrent workers processes 10k Cases in ~6 minutes cleanly. 500 workers processes them in hours with errors. Counter-intuitive but real.

For a sandbox org (only 5 concurrent limit): cap at 3-4.

RACE CONDITION QUESTION: IS IT ACTUALLY SOLVED?

Your concern: multiple workers writing simultaneously — do we get race conditions?

No, because each worker owns exclusive records. The Coordinator splits 10k IDs into 500 non-overlapping batches of 20. Worker 1 gets IDs 1-20, Worker 2 gets IDs 21-40, etc. They never touch the same Case IDs, never touch the same R2 keys, never write to the same D1 rows.

The only shared write is UPDATE jobs SET processed_records = processed_records + 20. D1 handles this fine — it's an atomic increment, SQLite serializes writes. No race condition.

Compare to your current architecture: multiple Lambdas all appending to the same email.json file per case. That's the actual race condition source.

COMPLETE ARCHITECTURE DIAGRAM

POST /api/jobs
    │
    ▼
Hono Worker
    │  Creates job in D1 (status='queued')
    │  Triggers CoordinatorWorkflow
    │  Returns { job_id } to frontend immediately
    ▼
CoordinatorWorkflow (1 per job, runs forever until complete)
    │
    ├─ Step 1: Resolve IDs
    │    If < 2k: REST SOQL synchronous
    │    If > 2k: Bulk API 2.0 Query Job (poll with step.sleep)
    │    Write all IDs to D1 job_records (INSERT OR IGNORE)
    │    Update D1 jobs.total_records
    │
    ├─ Step 2: Fan out
    │    Split IDs into batches of 20
    │    env.BATCH_QUEUE.sendBatch([
    │      { body: { job_id, org_id, case_ids: [id1..id20] } },
    │      { body: { job_id, org_id, case_ids: [id21..id40] } },
    │      ... × 500
    │    ])
    │    Update D1 jobs.status = 'running'
    │
    ├─ Step 3: Monitor (step.sleep loop)
    │    Every 60 seconds:
    │      SELECT COUNT(*) FROM job_records 
    │      WHERE job_id = ? AND status = 'pending'
    │    If 0 → proceed to finalize
    │    If still pending → step.sleep('wait', '60 seconds') → retry
    │
    └─ Step 4: Finalize
         Count failed records
         Bulk update SF status fields (Bulk API 2.0)
         Write billing_events
         Write audit_log
         Update D1 jobs.status = 'done'
         Send email notification

QUEUE: vault-batch-queue
  max_concurrency: 10  (configurable per tenant, sandbox gets 3)
  max_batch_size: 1    (one batch message per consumer invocation)
  max_retries: 3
  dead_letter_queue: vault-dlq

BatchWorker (queue consumer, 15 min wall time, no CPU limit)
    │
    ├─ 1. Check D1: are these IDs already archived? (INSERT OR IGNORE already ran)
    │       If all archived → ack immediately (idempotent retry)
    │
    ├─ 2. Get + validate SF token
    │
    ├─ 3. Composite API (1 call = 1 API unit):
    │       5 subrequests for 20 cases
    │       Returns: Cases, EmailMessages, ContentDocLinks, FeedItems, Comments
    │
    ├─ 4. Fetch ContentDocument metadata (1 REST call for all doc IDs)
    │
    ├─ 5. Write emails + feed to R2 (parallel, max 5 concurrent PUTs)
    │       Key per email: /{tenant}/{Case}/{case_id}/emails/{email_id}.json
    │       Idempotent PUTs
    │
    ├─ 6. Stream attachments to R2 (sequential, zero memory)
    │       fetch(sfUrl).body → R2.put(key, stream)
    │       Max 3 concurrent streams (memory safety)
    │
    ├─ 7. Verify (HEAD check or count check)
    │       If fails → UPDATE D1 job_records status='failed' for these IDs
    │                → ack (don't retry — log the failure)
    │
    ├─ 8. DELETE from SF (only if verify passed)
    │       EmailMessages: Composite DELETE batches of 25
    │       ContentDocuments: Composite DELETE batches of 25
    │
    ├─ 9. UPDATE D1
    │       job_records: status='archived' for all 20 IDs
    │       jobs: processed_records += 20 (atomic increment)
    │
    └─ 10. message.ack()

Dead Letter Queue worker (vault-dlq consumer):
    Reads failed messages
    Marks D1 job_records as 'failed' 
    Logs error details
    Updates jobs.failed_records count
HOW TO HANDLE SANDBOX vs PRODUCTION

When you create the org record in D1, store sf_type: 'sandbox' | 'production'. Queue consumer reads this and adjusts its behavior:

typescript
const MAX_CONCURRENT_SF_CALLS = org.sf_type === 'sandbox' ? 2 : 5

// When streaming attachments:
for (let i = 0; i < attachments.length; i += MAX_CONCURRENT_SF_CALLS) {
  await Promise.all(
    attachments.slice(i, i + MAX_CONCURRENT_SF_CALLS).map(stream)
  )
}
And the queue max_concurrency is set at the queue level — you'd want separate queues or a tenant-aware concurrency controller. Simplest approach: one queue, max_concurrency: 5 globally. For production orgs it's slightly conservative, for sandboxes it's exactly right.

SUMMARY: YOUR QUESTION ANSWERED DIRECTLY

Queue consumer runtime is indeed 15 minutes wall time with no CPU limit — you're right about that. And yes, the Workflow fans out into Queue for parallelism. But the parallel concurrency must be capped at ~10 (not 500) because the bottleneck is always the Salesforce org, not Cloudflare. 10 concurrent workers processing 10k records takes ~6 minutes cleanly. 500 workers causes the exact glitchy behavior you already have. The Workflow adds durability and coordination on top — it knows when all batches finish, handles the finalize step, and provides the progress the frontend reads.

Claude responded: Good thinking.
Good thinking. Let me think through this properly.

YOUR PROPOSED FLOW

10k Cases → Bulk API 2.0 Query → CSV in R2 → 
read CSV in chunks → queue batches → 
workers fetch related data (emails, attachments) → 
write to R2 → final Bulk API delete
The idea is sound in parts. Let me break down what works, what doesn't, and what the actual right version looks like.

WHAT BULK API 2.0 ACTUALLY RETURNS AND DOESN'T

This is the critical thing to understand first.

Bulk API 2.0 Query is for structured record data only — field values of SF objects. It returns CSV rows.

# What Bulk API 2.0 CAN fetch in one job:
SELECT Id, CaseNumber, Subject, Status, CreatedDate FROM Case WHERE ...
→ returns CSV: Id,CaseNumber,Subject,...
   0050x1,00001234,Invoice issue,Closed,...
   0050x2,00001235,Shipping delay,Closed,...

SELECT Id, ParentId, HtmlBody, TextBody, FromAddress, 
       ToAddress, MessageDate, Subject FROM EmailMessage 
WHERE ParentId IN (...)  ← PROBLEM: can't pass 10k IDs in WHERE IN clause

SELECT Id, ContentDocumentId, LinkedEntityId 
FROM ContentDocumentLink WHERE LinkedEntityId IN (...)  ← same problem
The WHERE IN problem is real. Bulk API query is just SOQL executed asynchronously. SOQL has a max query length. You cannot pass 10,000 Case IDs in a single WHERE IN clause. It will fail or get truncated.

Attachment binaries cannot come from Bulk API at all. Bulk API returns field values. ContentVersion binary data lives at a separate endpoint (/sobjects/ContentVersion/{id}/VersionData). You must fetch each binary individually via REST, streamed. No bulk equivalent exists for file content.

So your architecture is partially right but needs adjustment for these realities.

WHAT THE RIGHT ARCHITECTURE LOOKS LIKE

Split into three phases with different API strategies for each:

PHASE 1: RESOLVE — get all IDs, store in R2 as CSV
  Uses: Bulk API 2.0 Query (best tool, truly async, no timeout)
  Output: R2 CSV files of Case IDs + EmailMessage IDs + ContentDocumentLink IDs

PHASE 2: FETCH + ARCHIVE — get actual data, write to R2
  Uses: REST Composite API in controlled parallel queue workers
  Input: IDs from Phase 1 CSV
  Output: email JSONs + attachment binaries in R2

PHASE 3: DELETE + FINALIZE — remove from SF, update fields
  Uses: Bulk API 2.0 Ingest (delete) + Bulk API 2.0 Update
  Input: list of successfully archived IDs from D1
  Output: SF cleaned up, custom fields updated
This is where Bulk API actually saves you API calls — Phases 1 and 3. Phase 2 is unavoidably REST because you need to stream file binaries and Bulk doesn't do that.

PHASE 1 IN DETAIL — THE BULK QUERY CHAIN

Step 1a: Bulk Query for Case IDs
  POST /jobs/query
  { query: "SELECT Id FROM Case WHERE Status='Closed' AND ClosedDate < 2024-01-01" }
  → Job ID returned immediately
  → Poll until JobComplete
  → GET /jobs/query/{id}/results → CSV stream
  → Write entire CSV to R2: /{tenant}/{job_id}/phase1/case_ids.csv
  
  This is one Bulk API job. No REST API call limit consumed.
  Can return 10M records if needed.

Step 1b: Bulk Query for EmailMessage IDs + body fields
  HERE IS THE PROBLEM: you can't do WHERE ParentId IN (10k IDs)
  
  SOLUTION: use a subquery
  "SELECT Id, ParentId, HtmlBody, TextBody, Headers, Subject,
          FromName, FromAddress, ToAddress, CcAddress, BccAddress,
          MessageDate, CreatedDate, CreatedById, Incoming,
          ThreadIdentifier, IsExternallyVisible
   FROM EmailMessage 
   WHERE ParentId IN (
     SELECT Id FROM Case WHERE Status='Closed' AND ClosedDate < 2024-01-01
   )"
  
  Same filter as step 1a but applied to EmailMessage directly.
  SF executes the subquery server-side — no ID list in your query.
  → CSV stream → R2: /{tenant}/{job_id}/phase1/email_messages.csv
  
  This is ONE Bulk API job. Returns all email body fields.
  HtmlBody in CSV is the full email content — no separate fetch needed.

Step 1c: Bulk Query for ContentDocumentLink IDs
  "SELECT Id, ContentDocumentId, LinkedEntityId
   FROM ContentDocumentLink
   WHERE LinkedEntityId IN (
     SELECT Id FROM Case WHERE Status='Closed' AND ClosedDate < 2024-01-01
   )"
  → CSV → R2: /{tenant}/{job_id}/phase1/content_links.csv

Step 1d: Bulk Query for ContentDocument/Version metadata
  "SELECT Id, LatestPublishedVersionId, Title, FileExtension, ContentSize
   FROM ContentDocument
   WHERE Id IN (
     SELECT ContentDocumentId FROM ContentDocumentLink
     WHERE LinkedEntityId IN (
       SELECT Id FROM Case WHERE Status='Closed' AND ClosedDate < 2024-01-01
     )
   )"
  → CSV → R2: /{tenant}/{job_id}/phase1/content_documents.csv

Step 1e: Bulk Query for FeedItems
  "SELECT Id, ParentId, Body, CreatedDate, CreatedById, Type, IsRichText
   FROM FeedItem
   WHERE ParentId IN (
     SELECT Id FROM Case WHERE Status='Closed' AND ClosedDate < 2024-01-01
   )"
  → CSV → R2: /{tenant}/{job_id}/phase1/feed_items.csv
Result of Phase 1: 5 Bulk API jobs. Zero REST API daily limit consumed. Everything you need for archival (except attachment binaries) is already in R2 as CSVs.

This is the big win. You've fetched ALL email bodies, ALL metadata, ALL relationship IDs — using only Bulk API which doesn't count against the daily REST limit.

PHASE 2 IN DETAIL — PARALLEL QUEUE WORKERS

Now you have CSVs in R2. You don't need to call SF for email bodies or metadata — it's all local. The only SF calls you need in Phase 2 are for attachment binaries (ContentVersion body data — not fetchable via Bulk).

CoordinatorWorkflow reads phase1/case_ids.csv from R2
Splits into chunks of 50 Case IDs (not 20 — larger is fine now)
Pushes one queue message per chunk

Queue message payload:
{
  job_id,
  org_id,
  tenant_id,
  case_ids: ['id1'..'id50'],
  phase1_prefix: '/{tenant}/{job_id}/phase1/'
}

BatchWorker (queue consumer):
  
  1. Read from R2 CSVs (NOT from SF):
     Parse email_messages.csv → filter rows where ParentId IN case_ids
     Parse content_links.csv  → filter rows where LinkedEntityId IN case_ids
     Parse content_documents.csv → filter by ContentDocumentId IN links
     Parse feed_items.csv → filter by ParentId IN case_ids
     
     Zero SF API calls so far.
  
  2. Write email JSONs to R2 (from CSV data, no SF call):
     For each EmailMessage row from CSV:
       key = /{tenant}/Case/{case_id}/emails/{email_id}.json
       value = JSON from CSV fields (HtmlBody, TextBody, all fields)
       R2.put(key, JSON.stringify(emailObj))
  
  3. Write feed data to R2 (from CSV data):
     key = /{tenant}/Case/{case_id}/feed/items.json
  
  4. Write Case snapshot to R2 (from case_ids.csv which has Case fields):
     key = /{tenant}/Case/{case_id}/snapshot.json
  
  5. Stream attachment BINARIES from SF (only SF calls in Phase 2):
     For each ContentVersion ID:
       GET /sobjects/ContentVersion/{id}/VersionData
       → stream directly to R2 (zero memory)
     
     This is where concurrency control matters.
     Max 5 concurrent streams per worker.
     No Composite API needed here at all.
  
  6. UPDATE D1 job_records: status = 'archived' for these case_ids
  7. message.ack()
SF API calls per chunk in Phase 2: only the attachment binary fetches. If a Case has 3 attachments, that's 3 REST calls. A chunk of 50 Cases with avg 3 attachments = 150 REST calls. But these are fast streaming GET calls, not long-running requests — they complete in seconds and don't count against the concurrent long-running limit.

Concurrency: you can now run 20-30 concurrent queue workers safely because each worker makes only fast GET requests (not long Composite calls), and they're not querying SF for structured data at all.

PHASE 3 IN DETAIL — BULK DELETE AND UPDATE

Once all batches are processed (Coordinator monitors D1 until all job_records are archived):

Step 3a: Bulk API 2.0 DELETE EmailMessages
  Read all successfully archived email IDs from D1:
    SELECT sf_record_id FROM job_records 
    WHERE job_id = ? AND status = 'archived'
  
  Upload CSV to Bulk Delete job:
    POST /jobs/ingest { operation: 'delete', object: 'EmailMessage' }
    Upload CSV: Id\n{email_id1}\n{email_id2}\n...
  
  SF processes async. Poll until complete.
  Deletes all EmailMessages in one job regardless of count.
  
  NOTE: You don't have individual email IDs in job_records because
  job_records tracks at the Case level.
  
  SOLUTION: read email IDs from phase1/email_messages.csv in R2
  Filter by parent Case IDs that are now archived.
  Upload those email IDs to the Bulk delete job.

Step 3b: Bulk API 2.0 DELETE ContentDocuments
  Read ContentDocumentIds from phase1/content_documents.csv
  Filter by cases that are now archived
  Bulk delete job → deletes ContentDocument → cascades to ContentVersion + Links

Step 3c: Bulk API 2.0 UPDATE Cases
  Upload CSV:
    Id,vault__Is_Archived__c,vault__Archival_Status__c,vault__Archived_At__c
    {case_id1},true,Archived,2026-05-02T14:00:00Z
    {case_id2},true,Archived,2026-05-02T14:00:00Z
  
  One Bulk job. Updates all 10k Cases at once.
  
Step 3d: Cleanup R2 phase1 CSVs (optional)
  DELETE phase1/case_ids.csv
  DELETE phase1/email_messages.csv
  etc.
  
  OR keep them — they're useful for debugging and audit
  Cost is negligible (text CSVs, not binaries)
API CALL COUNT COMPARISON

OLD ARCHITECTURE (parallel Lambdas, REST only):
  10,000 Cases / 20 per batch = 500 batches
  Each batch: 1 Composite (5 subrequests) + ~60 file fetches
  + 1 Composite delete + 1 Composite update
  ≈ 500 × 3 calls = 1,500 REST API units used from daily limit
  Plus 500 × 60 file streams = 30,000 individual requests
  All hitting SF simultaneously → concurrent limit violations

NEW ARCHITECTURE (Bulk + Queue):
  Phase 1: 5 Bulk API query jobs → 0 REST API units consumed
  Phase 2: 200 chunks × 3 file fetches avg = 600 REST calls total
           (only fast streaming GETs, not long-running)
  Phase 3: 3 Bulk API jobs → 0 REST API units
  
  Total REST API daily limit consumed: ~600 units
  vs 1,500+ in old approach
  
  And the Bulk jobs handle unlimited scale — 100k Cases costs
  the same as 10k Cases in terms of API unit consumption.

FINALIZE:
  Count failed records from D1
  Write billing_events
  Write audit_log
  Update D1 jobs.status = 'done'
  Send notification
  Optionally clean up phase1 CSVs from R2
ONE THING TO WATCH

The phase1 CSV for email_messages can be large. 10k Cases × avg 6 emails × avg 20KB per email body = ~1.2GB CSV. Writing and reading this from R2 is fine (R2 handles it), but you need to stream it — never load the entire CSV into Worker memory.

When BatchWorker reads the email CSV, it uses a streaming CSV parser that processes row by row:

typescript
// WRONG: loads entire 1.2GB into memory
const csv = await env.R2.get(key).then(r => r.text())

// RIGHT: stream and parse row by row
const r2obj = await env.R2.get(key)
const stream = r2obj.body  // ReadableStream
// pipe through CSV parser, filter rows by case_ids set
// write matching rows to R2 immediately, never buffer all
This is the same streaming principle as attachment binaries — the Worker is always a pipe, never a buffer.

ANSWER TO YOUR QUESTION DIRECTLY

Yes, your instinct is correct. The right architecture is:

Bulk API to resolve everything into R2 CSVs (one query per object type using subqueries — no ID lists needed)
Queue workers that read from R2 CSVs (zero SF calls for structured data)
Workers only call SF for attachment binaries (unavoidable, but fast streaming GETs)
Bulk API to delete and update at the end
This reduces SF REST API consumption by ~80%, eliminates the concurrent limit problem for structured data fetching, and makes the queue workers much faster since they're mostly doing R2 reads and writes rather than waiting on SF API responses.

Good. Here's the complete breakdown.

R2 PRICING — THREE DIMENSIONS

Standard storage: $0.015/GB-month. Class A operations (writes, PUTs, lists): $4.50/million. Class B operations (reads, GETs): $0.36/million. Delete operations: free. 
Oreate AI

Free tier every month: 10GB storage, 1 million Class A operations, 10 million Class B operations. 
Oreate AI

Zero egress fees — reading data out never costs anything regardless of volume.

HOW STORAGE BILLING ACTUALLY WORKS

Storage is billed as GB-month, calculated by averaging peak storage per day over the billing period. Storing 1GB for 5 days then 3GB for remaining 25 days = 1×5/30 + 3×25/30 = 2.66 GB-month. 
Cloudflare

So if you store a 1GB temp CSV for 1 hour then delete it: 1GB × (1/720 hours in a month) = 0.0014 GB-month = $0.00002

Essentially free for temp files.

YOUR SPECIFIC COST: TEMP PHASE1 CSVS

For a 10k Case archive job:

Phase1 CSV sizes (realistic estimates):
  case_ids.csv           ~500KB   (10k rows, just IDs)
  email_messages.csv     ~500MB   (60k emails × avg 8KB body)
  content_links.csv      ~1MB     (links data, small)
  content_documents.csv  ~2MB     (metadata only)
  feed_items.csv         ~50MB    (feed text data)
  
  Total: ~554MB ≈ 0.55GB

Stored for: ~2 hours (Phase 1 + Phase 2 processing time)
Storage cost: 0.55GB × (2/720) = 0.0015 GB-month × $0.015 = $0.00002

Operations to write these 5 files: 5 Class A PUTs
Operations to read them (workers parsing): ~200 GET × 5 files = 1000 Class B
Cost: negligible (well within free tier)

Delete operations: free
The temp CSV storage for one job costs literally $0.00002. You could run 50,000 jobs before spending $1 on temp storage.

THE REAL R2 COST IN YOUR SYSTEM

The temp CSVs are not where you'll spend money. The permanent archived data is.

Per archived Case (stored permanently):
  Email JSONs:    avg 5 emails × 15KB = 75KB
  Attachments:    avg 3 files × 500KB = 1.5MB
  Metadata/feed:  ~10KB
  Total per case: ~1.6MB

10,000 Cases archived = 16GB stored permanently

Monthly storage cost: 16GB × $0.015 = $0.24/month

For a tenant with 100,000 Cases archived over time:
  160GB × $0.015 = $2.40/month in storage

Operations per month (users viewing archived content):
  100k Cases × 2 views/month avg = 200k Class B GETs
  200k / 1M × $0.36 = $0.07/month
Your total R2 cost for a tenant with 100k archived Cases: ~$2.50/month.

You're charging that tenant $149-499/month. R2 cost is under 2% of revenue.

SUMMARY TABLE

Temp Phase1 CSVs (hours of storage):   ~$0.00002 per job → ignore
Permanent email JSONs:                  $0.015/GB-month
Permanent attachment binaries:          $0.015/GB-month
Class A (writes during archive):        $4.50/million → ~$0.0005 per job
Class B (reads when viewing):           $0.36/million → ~$0.00003 per view
Egress (downloads, presigned URLs):     $0 always
Delete operations:                      $0 always
The only cost worth thinking about is long-term storage of the actual archived binaries — and at $0.015/GB that's dirt cheap compared to the Salesforce storage costs your customers were paying ($125/month per 500MB extra data storage).

A customer saving 10GB of Salesforce storage saves $250/month. That same 10GB in R2 costs you $0.15/month to store. Your margin on storage alone is 99.9%.

WHY I SAID 2 HOURS BEFORE — AND WHY I WAS WRONG

That estimate was for the sequential Workflow architecture where one worker processes 20 cases at a time, making Composite API calls for each chunk. With the new Bulk + Queue architecture the timing is completely different because:

Phase 1 (Bulk) fetches ALL structured data in parallel at SF's processing speed — you're not waiting for your own round trips
Phase 2 workers read from R2 CSVs (microseconds) and only hit SF for binary file streams
Phase 3 Bulk delete/update runs once for all 10k at once
So let me calculate actual timings.

PHASE 1 TIMING — BULK API QUERIES

SF processes Bulk jobs at roughly 1-2 million records per minute on the server side. Your 5 jobs run in parallel (all submitted before any polling begins).

Case IDs query (10k records):        ~15-30 seconds
EmailMessages query (60k records):   ~30-60 seconds  
ContentDocumentLinks (40k records):  ~20-40 seconds
ContentDocuments (30k records):      ~20-40 seconds
FeedItems (80k records):             ~30-60 seconds

All 5 submitted simultaneously, all polled in parallel.
Longest job finishes: ~60-90 seconds.

Phase 1 total: 1.5-2 minutes
Streaming results from SF to R2 after completion adds another 1-2 minutes depending on CSV size (email_messages.csv with HtmlBody fields can be 300-500MB).

Phase 1 total: 3-5 minutes regardless of whether you have 10k or 50k Cases.

PHASE 2 TIMING — THE REAL VARIABLE

Phase 2 splits into two completely different speed profiles:

Email JSONs and metadata — read from R2 CSV, write to R2. This is purely R2 read/write speed, no SF involved.

60k email JSONs written to R2
R2 PUT throughput: ~100-500 requests/second per Worker
25 concurrent workers × 100 PUTs/sec = 2,500 PUTs/sec
60k PUTs / 2,500/sec = 24 seconds

Email JSON archival: ~1-2 minutes total
Attachment binaries — this is the real variable. You stream from SF directly to R2.

The constraints are:

SF doesn't publish a per-second bandwidth limit but in practice a single org sustains roughly 50-150 MB/s total outbound across all connections
Files under ~20 seconds to transfer don't count as "long-running" requests so the concurrent limit of 25 doesn't apply
R2 PUT accepts a ReadableStream — no Worker memory involved, it's a pure pipe
Let me calculate for different scenarios:

SCENARIO A — Typical case (service desk, support team)
  Per Case: avg 6 emails, avg 3 attachments, avg 500KB each
  10k Cases × 3 files × 500KB = 15GB attachments
  
  Sustained SF bandwidth: ~80MB/s (shared org, realistic)
  15GB / 80MB/s = 188 seconds ≈ 3 minutes
  
  Phase 2 total: ~5 minutes

SCENARIO B — Medium case (contracts, proposals)
  Per Case: avg 8 emails, avg 8 attachments, avg 3MB each
  10k Cases × 8 files × 3MB = 240GB attachments
  
  Sustained SF bandwidth: ~80MB/s
  240GB / 80MB/s = 3,000 seconds ≈ 50 minutes
  
  Phase 2 total: ~55 minutes

SCENARIO C — Heavy case (your specific example: 15-20 files × 10MB)
  Per Case: 20 attachments × 10MB = 200MB per case
  10k Cases × 200MB = 2TB attachments
  
  Sustained SF bandwidth: ~80MB/s
  2TB / 80MB/s = 25,000 seconds ≈ 7 hours
  
  Phase 2 total: ~7 hours
For scenario C (2TB), this is a background overnight job. The user triggers it at 10pm, it's done by 5am. Nobody is waiting for it. The progress bar in your dashboard shows real-time progress from D1 throughout.

The architecture doesn't break under heavy load — it just takes longer. No timeouts, no retries, no errors. It just keeps streaming.

PHASE 3 TIMING — BULK DELETE AND UPDATE

Bulk delete 60k EmailMessages:    ~1-2 minutes SF processing
Bulk delete 30k ContentDocuments: ~1-2 minutes
Bulk update 10k Cases:            ~30-60 seconds

All 3 submitted simultaneously.
Phase 3 total: ~2 minutes
TOTAL JOB DURATION SUMMARY

Scenario A (15GB attachments):    ~10 minutes
Scenario B (240GB attachments):   ~60 minutes  
Scenario C (2TB attachments):     ~7.5 hours
The bottleneck is always SF outbound bandwidth for binaries. Nothing about your architecture, your Workers, or your R2 is the constraint.

R2 Storage (permanent archived data):
  Email JSONs: 60k × avg 15KB = 900MB
  Attachments: 240GB
  Total new permanent data: ~241GB per job
  Monthly storage cost for this data: 241GB × $0.015 = $3.62/month ongoing

Workflow steps per job:
  Phase 1 polls: ~20 steps
  Fan-out: ~10 steps  
  Monitoring polls: ~20 steps
  Finalize: ~5 steps
  Total: ~55 steps
  Cost: well within 10M free tier per month

D1 operations per job:
  10k INSERTs (job_records): within free tier (100k writes/day free)
  10k UPDATEs: within free tier
  Progress reads (frontend polling every 5s for 60min): 720 reads → free

Workers requests per job:
  Queue consumer invocations: 200 (one per batch of 50)
  Hono API calls (job create, status polls): ~200
  Total: ~400 requests → negligible
Per-job cost: ~$0.54 in R2 operations + ongoing $3.62/month in storage per job run

THE STORAGE COST GROWTH CURVE

This is the important long-term number to track. R2 storage accumulates as clients archive more data over time.

Per client archiving 100GB/month:
  Month 1:  100GB stored = $1.50/month R2
  Month 6:  600GB stored = $9/month R2
  Month 12: 1.2TB stored = $18/month R2
  Month 24: 2.4TB stored = $36/month R2

A Growth client paying $149/month:
  Your R2 cost for them at month 24: $36/month
  Your margin after R2: $113/month (76%)
  
  If they BYOC (bring their own R2): your margin: $149/month (100%)
  → This is why BYOC is a valuable Enterprise tier feature
The other cost consideration: what you're replacing. That same client had 1.2TB in Salesforce after year 1. At $5/GB/month for SF file storage: $6,000/month they were paying Salesforce. You charge them $149. They save $5,851/month. Your R2 cost is $18/month. The value created vs cost is a 325x ratio.

You said: one more thing , some org have triggers, flows on these records, or some have custom validations on records as well.
one more thing , some org have triggers, flows on these records, or some have custom validations on records as well. so how do we handle such things in place ? 

like i saw in one client org that when we restore data we recreate the case email message due to which a flow is triggered on there org making the case astatus as reopned 

one more thing we observed that one cliewnt org has many custom validation on opportunity obejct type , so when we try to update custom field status value using rest api it does not work and gives us error exception of field. 

these are edge cases, like if some flows take time or some trigger take time we will get read timeout error etc too so how we handle these things ?

May 2
Claude responded: These are real production problems.
These are real production problems. Let me think through each one properly.

THE THREE CATEGORIES OF SF ORG INTERFERENCE

1. Flows/Process Builders triggered on INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE
   → Case reopening on EmailMessage insert (your exact problem)
   → Welcome emails sent, notifications fired, etc.

2. Validation Rules on UPDATE
   → Required fields not being set
   → Field dependency rules
   → Cross-object validation
   → Your Opportunity custom field error

3. Apex Triggers (before/after insert/update/delete)
   → Slow triggers causing timeout
   → Triggers that throw exceptions
   → Triggers that modify related records causing cascade
PROBLEM 1: FLOWS TRIGGERED ON RESTORE

Your exact scenario: restore EmailMessage → Flow detects new EmailMessage on Case → sets Case.Status = 'Reopened'.

The root cause is SF doesn't distinguish between "this is a new customer email" and "this is a restore from archive". Everything looks the same to their automation.

Solution A: Bypass Flows with a sentinel field

During onboarding setup (Metadata API), you deploy one extra custom field:

EmailMessage.vault__Restore_In_Progress__c   Checkbox, default false
Case.vault__Restore_In_Progress__c           Checkbox, default false
When restoring:

First PATCH the Case: vault__Restore_In_Progress__c = true
INSERT the EmailMessages (with this parent Case having the sentinel = true)
PATCH the Case back: vault__Restore_In_Progress__c = false
The client's SF admin then adds one condition to their Flow entry criteria:

Flow entry criteria:
  AND Case.vault__Restore_In_Progress__c = false   ← add this
This is a one-time setup you guide the client through during onboarding. Show them exactly which flows to update with a screenshot. Most orgs have 1-3 flows touching EmailMessage — it's a 5-minute admin task.

Problem: you're asking the client to modify their flows. Some won't do it. Some can't (locked ISV flows).

Solution B: Disable Automation via API header

SF has a lesser-known feature: you can pass a request header that suppresses automation for your specific integration user.

POST /sobjects/EmailMessage
Headers:
  Authorization: Bearer {token}
  Sforce-Auto-Assign: FALSE
Sforce-Auto-Assign: FALSE suppresses assignment rules. But it doesn't suppress Flows or Triggers.

The real answer for flows: there's no universal API bypass for Flows. Salesforce intentionally doesn't provide one because Flows are considered business logic. The only real options are:

Option 1: Sentinel field approach (guided setup, client adds condition)
Option 2: Ask client to deactivate the specific flow during restore window
Option 3: Accept the side effect, detect it, and reverse it
Option 3 is the most resilient. Here's how:

Before restoring, you query the Case's current Status and store it:

GET /sobjects/Case/{id}?fields=Status,vault__Is_Archived__c
→ { Status: 'Closed', ... }
After restoring all EmailMessages, you check if Status changed and revert:

PATCH /sobjects/Case/{id}
{ Status: 'Closed' }  ← restore original value
This adds one extra API call per Case restore but handles the problem automatically with zero client involvement. The user never even sees the Case flicker to Reopened — the revert happens within the same job.

Store the original Status in your snapshot.json in R2 when archiving. You already have it. Use it on restore.

PROBLEM 2: VALIDATION RULES BLOCKING FIELD UPDATES

Your scenario: PATCH Case/Opportunity with vault__Archival_Status__c = 'Archived' → validation rule fires → FIELD_CUSTOM_VALIDATION_EXCEPTION.

This happens because validation rules run on every record save regardless of what field you're touching. If the rule says "Opportunity.Amount must be > 0 when Stage = Closed Won" and you PATCH just your custom field, SF re-evaluates ALL validations on that save. If the record was in a bad state before you touched it (legacy data, admin bypassed validation when creating it), you now get the error.

Solution: isolate your field updates from record saves

The cleanest approach is using a separate custom object to track archival status instead of putting it on Case/Opportunity directly.

vault__Archival_Record__c  (new custom object, no validation rules)
  vault__SF_Record_Id__c   Text(18), External ID, Unique
  vault__Object_Type__c    Text
  vault__Status__c         Picklist
  vault__Archived_At__c    DateTime
  vault__Job_Id__c         Text(36)
You create/update rows in this object. No validation rules ever run on it because it's your own object with no client customization. The Case/Opportunity itself is never touched during archive/restore status updates.

To query archived Cases, you join:

sql
SELECT Case.Id, vault__Archival_Record__c.vault__Status__c
FROM Case
WHERE Id IN (
  SELECT vault__SF_Record_Id__c 
  FROM vault__Archival_Record__c 
  WHERE vault__Status__c = 'Archived'
)
The tradeoff: you lose the native field on the Case page layout. Users can't filter Cases by Is_Archived__c directly. You'd need a formula field or a related list from the custom object.

Better hybrid solution: keep the custom fields on Case/Opportunity for display purposes, but update them using allOrNone: false with error catching at the record level:

javascript
// Composite PATCH with allOrNone: false
// If a record fails validation, log it, skip it
// Don't let one bad record fail the whole batch

const composite = {
  allOrNone: false,  // ← critical
  compositeRequest: batchOf25.map((caseId, i) => ({
    method: 'PATCH',
    url: `/services/data/v66.0/sobjects/Case/${caseId}`,
    referenceId: `update_${i}`,
    body: { vault__Archival_Status__c: 'Archived' }
  }))
}
For records that fail, you catch the FIELD_CUSTOM_VALIDATION_EXCEPTION specifically and:

Mark D1 job_records.sf_field_update_failed = true
The archive still happened (data is in R2, deleted from SF)
The Case just won't show the custom field value
You log this in the audit trail with the specific validation error
The user can see in your dashboard: "8,241 archived successfully, 3 field update failures (data still archived)"
PROBLEM 3: APEX TRIGGERS CAUSING TIMEOUTS

When your API call hits SF and a slow Apex trigger runs, you get a timeout. Your REST call returns a 504 or a REQUEST_RUNNING_TOO_LONG error.

The SF API timeout is hard-capped at 10 minutes per request. Apex triggers that take more than a few seconds are themselves violating SF governor limits (10 second CPU limit per transaction), so truly hanging triggers shouldn't exist — but slow ones that run 5-8 seconds per record × 25 records in a Composite call = real timeout risk.

Solution: exponential backoff + batch size reduction

typescript
async function patchWithRetry(
  url: string,
  body: object,
  attempt = 1
): Promise<Response> {
  
  const timeout = 30000 * attempt  // 30s, 60s, 90s
  
  try {
    const response = await fetch(url, {
      method: 'PATCH',
      headers: { Authorization: `Bearer ${token}` },
      body: JSON.stringify(body),
      signal: AbortSignal.timeout(timeout)
    })
    
    if (response.status === 504 || response.status === 429) {
      throw new Error(`SF timeout: ${response.status}`)
    }
    
    return response
    
  } catch (err) {
    if (attempt >= 3) throw err
    
    const delay = Math.min(1000 * Math.pow(2, attempt), 30000)
    await sleep(delay)
    return patchWithRetry(url, body, attempt + 1)
  }
}
For Composite calls where triggers are the issue, also reduce batch size:

typescript
// If org is known to have slow triggers, reduce from 25 to 5 per composite
const COMPOSITE_BATCH_SIZE = orgHasSlowTriggers ? 5 : 25
You detect "this org has slow triggers" by tracking response times in D1:

sql
ALTER TABLE sf_orgs ADD COLUMN avg_api_response_ms INTEGER DEFAULT 0;
ALTER TABLE sf_orgs ADD COLUMN trigger_slowness_detected INTEGER DEFAULT 0;
If 3+ timeouts occur in a job for the same org, set trigger_slowness_detected = 1, reduce batch size for all future operations on that org.

PROBLEM 4: DELETE TRIGGERS SIDE EFFECTS

When you delete EmailMessages, some orgs have before delete or after delete triggers that update the parent Case, send notifications, write to custom objects, etc. These can cause:

The Case status changing (same reopening problem but on delete not insert)
Related records getting orphaned
Other custom objects that reference the EmailMessage ID breaking
Solution: pre-delete snapshot and post-delete verification

Before any delete, store in your snapshot.json:

json
{
  "pre_delete_case_status": "Closed",
  "pre_delete_case_custom_fields": { ... }
}
After delete job completes, verify Case status hasn't changed. If it has, revert using stored snapshot. This is already partially handled by your Phase 3 Bulk Update that sets your custom fields — that PATCH after delete will overwrite any status change the delete trigger caused, as long as you also include Status: originalStatus in the PATCH payload.

PROBLEM 5: VALIDATION ON BULK API

Bulk API has a different validation behavior than REST API. By default, Bulk API 2.0 uses allOrNone: false at the record level — individual record failures don't fail the whole job. You get a results CSV back with success/failure per row.

For your Bulk update of Case fields after archiving:

POST /jobs/ingest
{
  "object": "Case",
  "operation": "update",
  "lineEnding": "LF"
}

Upload CSV:
Id,vault__Archival_Status__c,vault__Archived_At__c
0050x001,Archived,2026-05-02T14:00:00Z
0050x002,Archived,2026-05-02T14:00:00Z  ← this one has validation issue
0050x003,Archived,2026-05-02T14:00:00Z

Results CSV from SF:
sf__Id,sf__Created,sf__Error
0050x001,false,""          ← success
0050x002,false,"FIELD_CUSTOM_VALIDATION_EXCEPTION: ..."  ← failed
0050x003,false,""          ← success
Parse the results CSV. For failed records, log the specific error in D1 job_records.sf_field_update_error. The archive happened. The data is safe in R2. Just the SF field didn't update.

PUTTING IT ALL TOGETHER: THE RESILIENCE LAYER

Every operation that touches SF needs a result type that distinguishes between:

typescript
type SFOperationResult = 
  | { success: true }
  | { success: false; type: 'validation'; error: string; retryable: false }
  | { success: false; type: 'timeout'; retryable: true }
  | { success: false; type: 'rate_limit'; retryable: true; retryAfter: number }
  | { success: false; type: 'lock'; retryable: true }  // UNABLE_TO_LOCK_ROW
  | { success: false; type: 'unknown'; error: string; retryable: false }
Your BatchWorker handles each differently:

validation error  → log it, continue, mark job_record with error note
                    data is still archived, just status field didn't update
                    
timeout           → exponential backoff, retry up to 3 times
                    if still failing, reduce batch size and retry
                    
rate_limit        → step.sleep for retryAfter seconds, then retry
                    (SF sometimes returns Retry-After header)
                    
lock error        → wait 2-4 seconds, retry up to 5 times
                    (record locked by another transaction, transient)
                    
unknown           → log full error + request body for debugging
                    mark as failed, continue to next record
Add one column to D1 job_records:

sql
ALTER TABLE job_records ADD COLUMN sf_side_effects_json TEXT;
-- stores: { status_reverted: true, field_update_failed: true, error: "..." }
Your dashboard then shows the full picture:

Job #4821 Complete
✓ 8,238 Cases archived and SF updated
⚠  3 Cases archived but SF field update failed (validation rule)
ℹ  12 Cases: status reverted after restore (Flow detected and reversed)
✗  0 Cases failed entirely
ONBOARDING CHECKLIST ADDITION

Add two items to the onboarding checklist you already walk clients through:

□ (Optional but recommended) Add vault__Restore_In_Progress__c 
  condition to any Flows that trigger on EmailMessage creation
  [Link to guide with screenshots]

□ Ensure your integration user's Permission Set has:
  "Bypass Validation Rules" — if available on your org edition
  (available in some Enterprise orgs, not all)
  [Link to how to check]
For "Bypass Validation Rules" — this is a system permission available in some SF editions. If the client can grant it to your integration user, validation rules simply don't run for that user's operations. This solves the validation problem entirely without any changes to their rules.

Integration User Permission Set:
  ✓ API Enabled
  ✓ Bulk API Hard Delete  
  ✓ Set Audit Fields upon Record Creation
  ✓ Bypass Validation Rules  ← request this if available
  ✓ Modify All Data (scoped to Case, Opportunity, EmailMessage)
Not all clients will have this permission available or be willing to grant it, so your code still needs to handle validation failures gracefully. But for clients that do grant it, you get zero interference from their validation rules.

Claude responded: Two separate questions.

Thinking about admin authentication requirements and audit trail visibility in Salesforce web server OAuth flows
Thinking about admin authentication requirements and audit trail visibility in Salesforce web server OAuth flows
Two separate questions. Let me answer both clearly.

QUESTION 1: DOES WEB SERVER FLOW REQUIRE AN ADMIN?

No. But it needs to be the right user. Let me explain the distinction.

Web Server Flow — someone logs in interactively via SF login page, approves your app, you get their tokens. All API calls then run as that person. Their profile + permission sets determine what the API can do.

The person who does this does NOT need to be a System Administrator. They need to be a SF user with:

Sufficient permissions for what your app needs to do (delete EmailMessages, update Cases, etc.)
The Approve Uninstalled Connected Apps permission (since September 2025) to authorize your Connected App the first time
In practice your onboarding says: "Have your Salesforce admin connect the org." Admins have both. But technically any user with the right permission set could do it.

The real question you're asking is: whose name shows up when the API does things?

Answer: whoever logged in via Web Server flow. If the admin john.admin@client.com did the OAuth handshake, every API call — archive, delete, update — shows as John Admin in SF logs. When John leaves the company and his account is deactivated, your refresh token stops working. The org connection breaks.

This is why Web Server Flow alone is the wrong approach for background processing.

THE RIGHT TWO-USER ARCHITECTURE

The best practice is a single dedicated integration user per integrating system. All actions taken by the integration run as this user in Salesforce, and its data access and CRUD rights are scoped to only what is needed. This ensures auditability and traceability. 
Salesforce Developers

Here's how it actually works in production:

TWO SEPARATE THINGS:

1. ONBOARDING (Web Server Flow — one time, human admin)
   
   Admin clicks "Connect Salesforce" in your dashboard
   → Standard OAuth redirect → SF login
   → Admin logs in as themselves
   → Approves your Connected App
   → You get a temporary code
   → You exchange it for access_token + refresh_token
   → Store in D1
   
   Purpose: proves the admin authorized your app
   Used for: the initial setup only — deploying custom fields,
             Visualforce page, button via Metadata API

2. BACKGROUND PROCESSING (Client Credentials Flow — all jobs)
   
   Client's SF admin creates a dedicated Integration User:
     username: vault-integration@clientdomain.com
     profile: Minimum Access - API Only
     permission set: VaultIntegration (your custom set)
   
   Admin assigns this user as the "Run As" user 
   on your Connected App in their SF org
   
   All archive/restore/delete jobs run as this user
   Every API call shows vault-integration@clientdomain.com in SF logs
To enable the client credentials flow, go to the Manage view of the Connected App, change the permitted user policy to Admin Pre-Approved, and specify the Run-As User. All actions taken by the integration using the client credentials flow run as this user. 
Salesforce Developers

QUESTION 2: WHO SHOWS IN SF AUDIT LOGS WHEN YOU DELETE/UPDATE?

When your integration deletes an EmailMessage or updates a Case, SF records this in:

LastModifiedBy / CreatedBy on records — shows the integration user (vault-integration@client.com), not the admin who connected the app, not any real human. This is the username attached to the token you used.

Setup Audit Trail — shows configuration changes (custom field deployment etc.), attributed to whoever ran that operation.

Field History (if enabled on Case) — every field change tracked. Shows LastModifiedBy = vault-integration@clientdomain.com and exact timestamp. If your job updated vault__Archival_Status__c from blank to Archived, it's logged here.

Recycle Bin — deleted records sit here for 15 days. The "Deleted By" column shows vault-integration@clientdomain.com.

So from SF's perspective, a dedicated named user did everything. Not a mystery. Not an anonymous API. A traceable user with a meaningful name.

WHAT THIS LOOKS LIKE IN PRACTICE

A Salesforce admin in the client org opens Setup and looks at a Case's field history:

Field History for Case #00012345
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Date         User                        Field                  Old    New
May 2, 14:32 vault-integration@acme.com  vault__Is_Archived__c  -      true
May 2, 14:32 vault-integration@acme.com  vault__Archived_At__c  -      5/2/2026
They look at the recycle bin for deleted EmailMessages:

Recycle Bin
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Record Name       Type          Deleted By                    Date
Re: Invoice Issue EmailMessage  vault-integration@acme.com   May 2, 2026 2:32 PM
FWD: Order #441   EmailMessage  vault-integration@acme.com   May 2, 2026 2:32 PM
They don't see "some anonymous API". They see a named user. If they want to know who triggered the archive job that caused this, they look in your dashboard's audit log — which shows the human who clicked "Start Archive":

Your dashboard audit log:
May 2, 2:30 PM  j.smith@acme.com  Archive  8,241 Cases  Job #4821
This is actually better attribution than your current system. SF shows the integration user. Your dashboard shows the human. Both logs exist. Full traceability.

ONBOARDING STEPS FOR THE CLIENT (REVISED)

Step 1: Admin connects org via Web Server OAuth
        → One-time setup, deploys custom fields + button
        → Tokens stored but only used for Metadata API operations

Step 2: Admin creates dedicated Integration User in their SF org
        Setup → Users → New User
        Username: vault-integration@[theirdomain].com
        Profile: Minimum Access – API Only Integrations
        (Salesforce provides this profile specifically for integrations)

Step 3: Assign your Permission Set to that Integration User
        VaultIntegration permission set includes:
        - API Enabled
        - Bulk API Hard Delete
        - Set Audit Fields upon Record Creation
        - Read/Delete on EmailMessage
        - Read/Delete on ContentVersion, ContentDocument
        - Read/Edit on Case (for custom fields)
        - Read/Edit on Opportunity (for custom fields)

Step 4: In SF Setup → Connected App OAuth Usage → Vault
        → Edit → Set Run-As User → vault-integration@[domain]
        
Step 5: In your dashboard, click "Activate Integration User"
        → Your backend calls Client Credentials flow
        → Gets token running as that specific integration user
        → Stores in D1 separately from the admin's web server token
        → Tests connection with a simple API call
        → Shows green checkmark: "Integration user connected"
From this point forward, all archive/restore/delete jobs use the Client Credentials token. The admin's Web Server token is only used if you need to do Metadata API operations (deploying new fields, updating page layouts) because Metadata API often requires a real admin user, not just an API-only integration user.

TOKEN STORAGE IN D1 — TWO SEPARATE ROWS

sql
sf_tokens table:
  
  org_id: 'org-abc123'
  token_type: 'admin_web_server'   ← admin's token, for Metadata API
  access_token_enc: [encrypted]
  refresh_token_enc: [encrypted]
  sf_user_id: '005admin...'
  
  org_id: 'org-abc123'
  token_type: 'integration'        ← integration user, for all data ops
  access_token_enc: [encrypted]
  client_id_enc: [encrypted]       ← for client credentials re-auth
  client_secret_enc: [encrypted]
  sf_user_id: '005vault-int...'
Every archive/restore/delete Workflow step uses token_type = 'integration'. Metadata API setup calls use token_type = 'admin_web_server'.

SUMMARY

Web Server Flow doesn't require a System Admin profile specifically — but needs a user with sufficient permissions, and in practice that's an admin during onboarding. For all actual data operations (archive, delete, restore), you use Client Credentials flow running as a dedicated integration user. SF shows that integration user's name on every field history entry, deleted record, and audit log. Your dashboard shows the human who triggered it. Both logs together give complete traceability. Nobody sees an anonymous API — they see vault-integration@theirdomain.com which is unambiguous and auditable.

Claude responded: You're right, and this is an important distinction.

Searched the web
Searched the web
You're right, and this is an important distinction. Let me clarify exactly what page shows what.

THE TWO COMPLETELY DIFFERENT PLACES IN SF SETUP

There are two separate places in Salesforce that both have "Connected App" in their name but do completely different things:

PLACE 1: Setup → Connected Apps → Connected Apps OAuth Usage
  Shows: apps that users have authorized in this org
  Actions available: Install | Block
  You CANNOT edit the Run-As user here
  This is for the CLIENT's admin to manage what apps 
  are allowed in their org
  You are right — no Edit option here

PLACE 2: Setup → Apps → App Manager  
  Shows: apps that are DEFINED in this org (you built them here)
  Actions available: View | Edit | Manage
  The "Manage" → "Edit Policies" path is where Run-As user lives
  This is where the Connected App OWNER configures it
The problem: for Client Credentials flow, the Run-As user configuration lives in Place 2, which is only accessible if your Connected App is defined (installed) in that org. When you're a third-party SaaS, your Connected App is NOT defined in the client's org — it lives in your own developer org. So the client's admin cannot go to App Manager and see your app there to edit its policies.

HOW IT ACTUALLY WORKS FOR A THIRD-PARTY SAAS

Installed apps are formally added to an org by an admin. Uninstalled apps appear only in Connected Apps OAuth Usage with an Install button. 
Concretio

Your Connected App definition lives in your Cloudflare/developer environment or your own SF developer org. The client's org only sees it as an authorized app after OAuth.

So the real question is: how does the client set the Run-As integration user when they don't own the Connected App definition?

The answer is they don't set it at the Connected App level. The Client Credentials flow works differently for an external SaaS:

YOUR APP (you own the Connected App definition):
  Your Connected App has a consumer key + consumer secret
  Client Credentials flow uses YOUR key/secret to get a token
  
  But whose user does the token run as?
  
  → Whatever user performed the Web Server OAuth onboarding
  → That's the user whose permissions are used
  → That's the user who shows in SF logs
For Client Credentials to run as a specific integration user, the Connected App must be formally installed in the client's org AND the admin must configure the Run-As user inside App Manager. That requires the app to be in their App Manager, which means deploying it as a managed package or via Metadata API as an installed Connected App.

From the connected app detail page, click Manage, then Edit Policies. Under Client Credentials Flow, for Run As, find the user to assign. This page is only accessible when the Connected App is defined/installed in that org. 
Sfdc-lightning

THE PRACTICAL ANSWER: USE WEB SERVER FLOW WITH A DEDICATED USER

For a third-party SaaS selling to multiple clients, the cleanest real-world approach is:

1. Client creates a dedicated integration user in their SF org:
   vault-integration@theirdomain.com
   Profile: Minimum Access – API Only
   Permission Set: VaultIntegration (your required permissions)

2. Client's admin does Web Server OAuth onboarding AS that 
   integration user (logs in as vault-integration@..., not as themselves)
   
   OR:
   
   Admin does OAuth as themselves first (to get setup done),
   then you have admin guide: "create integration user, then reconnect
   as that user for ongoing operations"

3. You store the refresh_token for vault-integration@...
   All archive/restore/delete jobs use this token
   SF logs show vault-integration@theirdomain.com for everything
The key insight: Web Server flow gives you a token for whichever user logs in. So if the integration user logs in and authorizes your app, you get a refresh_token that always runs as them. No Run-As configuration needed anywhere. No App Manager access needed.

YOUR ONBOARDING FLOW REVISED

Step 1 — Admin connects for setup (their own account):
  Admin clicks "Connect Salesforce"
  → OAuth redirect → Admin logs in as themselves
  → Token stored as 'admin_setup' token
  → Used only for: Metadata API to deploy fields/buttons/VF page
  → One-time only

Step 2 — Create integration user (guide with screenshots):
  Your dashboard shows:
  "Please create a dedicated integration user in your Salesforce org.
   This user will perform all archive and restore operations.
   
   Recommended settings:
   - Username: vault-integration@[yourdomain].com
   - Profile: Minimum Access – API Only Integrations
   - Permission Set: [link to what they need]
   
   [I've created the integration user →]"

Step 3 — Connect integration user:
  Admin clicks "Connect Integration User"
  → OAuth redirect → Login page appears
  → Admin logs in AS vault-integration@... 
    (or delegates: admin uses "Log in as another user" SF feature)
  → Token stored as 'integration' token
  → All jobs use this token from now on

Step 4 — Verify:
  Your backend makes a test API call with integration token
  Checks the response: whoami shows vault-integration@...
  Shows green: "Integration user connected: vault-integration@..."
The "Log in as another user" feature in SF lets a System Admin temporarily act as another user. Admin can use this to authorize your app as the integration user without needing to know the integration user's password.

WHAT SHOWS IN SF AFTER THIS

Field History on Case #00012345:
  vault__Is_Archived__c changed by: vault-integration@acme.com ✓

Recycle Bin — deleted EmailMessages:
  Deleted By: vault-integration@acme.com ✓

  vault-integration@acme.com connected via OAuth
  via your Connected App ✓

Your dashboard audit log:
  j.smith@acme.com triggered Archive of 8,241 Cases at 2:30 PM ✓
Two separate clean trails. SF shows the integration user (machine identity). Your dashboard shows the human (business accountability).

SUMMARY

You're right — Connected Apps OAuth Usage only shows Install/Block. The Edit Policies / Run-As user configuration in App Manager is only available if the Connected App is installed/defined in that org, which it won't be for an external SaaS. The correct approach for an external SaaS is: client creates a dedicated integration user, that user logs in via the normal Web Server OAuth flow, you store their refresh token. All API calls run as that user. No App Manager access needed, no Run-As config needed, works on every SF edition and edition.

Investigated JWT Bearer flow viability for integration user authentication
Investigated JWT Bearer flow viability for integration user authentication
Yes. You're thinking about this correctly. Let me lay it all out precisely.

YOUR INSIGHT IS RIGHT

When the admin does the Web Server OAuth flow during onboarding, your Connected App gets installed in their org. After that installation, JWT Bearer flow becomes available for any user that has been pre-authorized against that Connected App. So you only need one interactive login ever — the initial admin onboarding — and after that, JWT Bearer lets you get tokens for any pre-approved user by just knowing their username.

HOW JWT BEARER FLOW WORKS

You hold a private key (stored in Cloudflare secrets). You uploaded the corresponding public certificate to your Connected App definition when you built it. That's a one-time thing you do once for your entire product, not per client.

When you want a token for vault-integration@client.com:

1. You build a JWT payload:
   {
     "iss": "your_consumer_key",          ← your Connected App's key
     "sub": "vault-integration@client.com", ← username they gave you
     "aud": "https://login.salesforce.com",
     "exp": now + 180 seconds             ← 3 minute window
   }

2. You sign it with YOUR private key (RSA-256)

3. POST to SF:
   https://login.salesforce.com/services/oauth2/token
   grant_type=urn:ietf:params:oauth:grant-type:jwt-bearer
   assertion={signed_jwt}

4. SF verifies the signature using the certificate 
   you uploaded to the Connected App
   
5. SF returns:
   { "access_token": "...", "instance_url": "..." }
   
   No refresh token. Token lasts ~2 hours.
   You just regenerate whenever it expires — same call.
No password. No interactive login. No stored credentials from the client. You only need their username string.

JWT Bearer can be used to act on behalf of a user without requiring user authorization, as long as: the user has logged into Salesforce at least once, and the user is pre-authorized by an admin via the Connected App's Permitted Users policy set to "Admin approved users are pre-authorized" with the user's profile or permission set added. 
Medium

THE COMPLETE ONBOARDING FLOW WITH JWT

STEP 1 — Admin does Web Server OAuth (one time, admin's own account)
  Purpose: installs your Connected App in their org
           runs Metadata API to deploy custom fields, button, VF page
  Who logs in: the SF admin themselves
  What you store: admin's access_token + refresh_token
                  (only used for Metadata API operations)
  
STEP 2 — Admin creates integration user (your dashboard guides them)
  Your dashboard shows a form: "Enter your integration user's username"
  Admin fills in: vault-integration@theirdomain.com
  You store this username in D1: sf_orgs.integration_username
  
  In their SF org, admin:
  - Creates the user (Setup → Users → New User)
  - Assigns VaultIntegration permission set to that user
  - Assigns that user's profile to your Connected App's permitted users
    (Setup → App Manager → Vault → Manage → Edit Policies → 
     Permitted Users: Admin approved → Manage Profiles → 
     add the integration user's profile OR permission set)
  
  That's it. No password shared. No interactive login as that user.

STEP 3 — You verify JWT works
  Your backend builds JWT with sub = vault-integration@theirdomain.com
  Calls SF token endpoint
  If it returns access_token → integration user is connected → ✓
  If it returns error → guide admin to complete step 2 correctly

FROM THIS POINT: all archive/restore/delete jobs use JWT Bearer
  Every job: sign JWT → get fresh access_token → use for API calls
  Token expires → sign new JWT → get new token → continue
  No stored secrets from client side at all
WHAT YOU STORE IN D1

sql
sf_orgs table:
  integration_username TEXT   ← "vault-integration@client.com"
  sf_instance_url TEXT        ← "https://client.my.salesforce.com"
  sf_type TEXT                ← 'production' | 'sandbox'

sf_tokens table (simplified now):
  org_id TEXT
  token_type TEXT             ← 'admin_setup' | 'integration_jwt'
  
  For admin_setup:
    access_token_enc TEXT     ← encrypted
    refresh_token_enc TEXT    ← encrypted
  
  For integration_jwt:
    NO token stored at rest
    Generated fresh on demand using username + your private key
    Cache current access_token in memory during job
    access_token_cache TEXT   ← optional short-term cache, expires 2hr
    access_token_expires_at INTEGER
Your private RSA key is stored in Cloudflare secrets (environment variable), not in D1. It never touches the database.

TOKEN GENERATION IN HONO/WORKER

typescript
import { SignJWT, importPKCS8 } from 'jose'

async function getSFAccessToken(
  org: SFOrg,
  env: Env
): Promise<{ accessToken: string; instanceUrl: string }> {
  
  // Check cache first
  if (org.accessTokenCache && org.accessTokenExpiresAt > Date.now() + 60000) {
    return { accessToken: org.accessTokenCache, instanceUrl: org.sfInstanceUrl }
  }
  
  // Build and sign JWT
  const privateKey = await importPKCS8(env.SF_PRIVATE_KEY, 'RS256')
  
  const jwt = await new SignJWT({})
    .setProtectedHeader({ alg: 'RS256' })
    .setIssuer(env.SF_CONSUMER_KEY)           // your Connected App key
    .setSubject(org.integrationUsername)       // vault-integration@client.com
    .setAudience(
      org.sfType === 'sandbox' 
        ? 'https://test.salesforce.com'
        : 'https://login.salesforce.com'
    )
    .setExpirationTime('3m')                   // SF requires within 3 min
    .sign(privateKey)
  
  // Exchange for access token
  const response = await fetch(
    `${org.sfInstanceUrl}/services/oauth2/token`,
    {
      method: 'POST',
      headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/x-www-form-urlencoded' },
      body: new URLSearchParams({
        grant_type: 'urn:ietf:params:oauth:grant-type:jwt-bearer',
        assertion: jwt
      })
    }
  )
  
  const data = await response.json()
  
  if (!response.ok) {
    throw new Error(`JWT auth failed: ${data.error_description}`)
  }
  
  // Cache for ~2 hours
  await db.update(sfOrgs)
    .set({ 
      accessTokenCache: data.access_token,
      accessTokenExpiresAt: Date.now() + 7200000
    })
    .where(eq(sfOrgs.id, org.id))
  
  return { accessToken: data.access_token, instanceUrl: data.instance_url }
}
Every archive Workflow step calls this at the start. If cache is fresh, zero network call. If expired, one fast POST to SF. Takes ~200ms.

THE ONE PREREQUISITE: PRIOR APPROVAL

JWT Bearer flow requires prior approval of the Connected App. The user must have a previous approval that includes a refresh token, OR the Connected App must be set to Admin-approved users are pre-authorized with the user's profile/permission set added. 
Apex Hours

The "Admin approved users are pre-authorized" route is what you use. The admin configures this in App Manager → your Connected App → Manage → Edit Policies → Permitted Users: Admin approved → then adds the integration user's permission set or profile.

This is the one step the admin must do after creating the integration user. Your onboarding checklist guides them through it with screenshots. Once done, JWT Bearer works forever for that user with no further interaction.

SANDBOX VS PRODUCTION

One important difference:

Production org:
  aud: "https://login.salesforce.com"
  
Sandbox org:
  aud: "https://test.salesforce.com"
You already store sf_type in D1. Use it to set the right audience. Wrong audience = JWT error.

SUMMARY

Yes — your thinking is exactly right. The flow is:

Admin does Web Server OAuth once (installs your app, runs setup)
Admin creates integration user, gives you their username
Admin pre-authorizes that user against your Connected App in App Manager
You use JWT Bearer from that point on — sign a JWT with their username + your private key → get access token → make API calls
No password ever stored, no refresh token needed, token regenerated on demand
This is the most secure, most resilient production pattern. The private key lives in Cloudflare secrets. The client never shares credentials. If the integration user is deactivated you get a clean error telling you exactly what happened. Certificate rotation is entirely under your control.

markdown
# Vault — Salesforce Storage Archival SaaS

## What We're Building
Multi-tenant SaaS platform that archives Salesforce email bodies and 
file attachments to Cloudflare R2, reducing Salesforce storage costs.
Customers connect their SF org, archive old Case/Opportunity data, 
view archived content from our dashboard, and restore when needed.

## Tech Stack (non-negotiable)
- **Frontend/Marketing**: Next.js (App Router), OpenNext, Cloudflare Pages
- **API**: Hono on Cloudflare Workers
- **Database**: Cloudflare D1 with Drizzle ORM
- **Storage**: Cloudflare R2
- **Queue**: Cloudflare Queues
- **Workflow**: Cloudflare Workflows
- **Auth (platform)**: Lucia v3 — email/password + Google OAuth
- **Billing**: Stripe (metered usage)
- **Language**: TypeScript everywhere, strict mode, no `any`
- **Package manager**: pnpm workspaces (monorepo)

## Monorepo Structure
vault/
apps/
web/          ← Next.js (marketing + dashboard)
worker/       ← Hono API + Workflows + Queue consumers
packages/
db/           ← Drizzle schema + migrations (D1)
types/        ← shared TypeScript types
sf-client/    ← Salesforce API client (JWT Bearer, Bulk API, Composite)


## Code Rules
- Arrow functions everywhere, no `function` keyword except exports
- Zod for all I/O validation (API request bodies, SF API responses, env vars)
- No `any`. No `as unknown as X`. Use proper types.
- Drizzle for all D1 queries — no raw SQL strings
- All env vars validated at startup with Zod
- Error types: never throw strings, always typed Error subclasses
- Every async function has explicit return type annotation

## Architecture Decisions (do not change without asking)

### Authentication — Two separate auth flows:
1. **Platform auth** (Lucia): users logging into our dashboard
2. **SF integration auth** (JWT Bearer flow): getting SF access tokens for background jobs
   - We hold ONE RSA private key (in CF secrets as SF_PRIVATE_KEY)
   - We hold ONE consumer key (SF_CONSUMER_KEY)
   - Client gives us their integration username
   - We sign a JWT → exchange for SF access token
   - No passwords stored. No refresh tokens. Regenerate on demand.
   - Token cached in D1 for ~1.5 hours, regenerated before expiry

### Onboarding Flow:
1. Admin does Web Server OAuth (installs our Connected App in their org,
   runs Metadata API setup — deploys custom fields + button)
2. Admin creates dedicated integration user in their SF org
3. Admin pre-authorizes that user on our Connected App (App Manager → 
   Manage → Edit Policies → Permitted Users: Admin approved → add profile)
4. Admin enters integration username in our dashboard
5. We test JWT Bearer → confirm working → org is ready

### Archive Job Flow (3 phases):
**Phase 1 — Bulk Resolve** (in CoordinatorWorkflow):
- 5 Bulk API 2.0 Query jobs submitted simultaneously
- Queries: Case IDs, EmailMessages (with HtmlBody), ContentDocumentLinks,
  ContentDocuments, FeedItems — all using SOQL subqueries (no ID lists)
- Results streamed to R2 as CSV: /{tenant}/{job_id}/phase1/*.csv
- All Case IDs written to D1 job_records (INSERT OR IGNORE)

**Phase 2 — Parallel Archive** (Queue workers, max_concurrency: 10):
- CoordinatorWorkflow fans out into CF Queue (50 Case IDs per message)
- BatchWorker reads email/metadata from R2 CSVs (zero SF API calls)
- Only SF calls: streaming attachment binaries (ContentVersion body)
  → streamed directly SF → R2 (ReadableStream, zero Worker memory)
- Max 5 concurrent attachment streams per worker (memory safety)
- Updates D1 job_records on completion

**Phase 3 — Bulk Delete + Update** (in CoordinatorWorkflow):
- Bulk API 2.0 DELETE EmailMessages (IDs from phase1 CSV)
- Bulk API 2.0 DELETE ContentDocuments (cascades to versions + links)
- Bulk API 2.0 UPDATE Cases (set custom fields: Is_Archived, Archived_At)
- All 3 submitted simultaneously, polled until complete

### R2 Storage Layout:
/{tenant_id}/
/phase1/{job_id}/        ← temp CSVs, deleted after phase 3
case_ids.csv
email_messages.csv     ← includes HtmlBody fields
content_links.csv
content_documents.csv
feed_items.csv
/Case/{case_id}/         ← permanent archived data
/emails/{email_id}.json
/attachments/{cv_id}
/attachments/{cv_id}.meta.json
/feed/items.json
/snapshot.json
/Opportunity/{opp_id}/
... same structure


### SF Custom Fields (deployed via Metadata API on setup):
On Case and Opportunity:
- vault__Is_Archived__c (Checkbox)
- vault__Archival_Status__c (Picklist: Pending/Archived/Failed)
- vault__Archived_At__c (DateTime)
- vault__Archive_Job_Id__c (Text 36)

Also deploys:
- Custom Button on Case: "View Archived Content" → our dashboard URL
- CSP Trusted Site: vault.com

### Edge Cases to Always Handle:
- SF Validation Rules: use `allOrNone: false` on all Composite calls,
  catch FIELD_CUSTOM_VALIDATION_EXCEPTION, log to D1, never abort job
- Flow side effects on restore: before restoring, snapshot Case.Status,
  after restore check if changed, revert if so
- Concurrent SF limit: max_concurrency: 10 on Queue (SF allows ~25 
  concurrent long-running requests, we stay well below)
- Sandbox orgs: use test.salesforce.com for JWT audience,
  reduce max_concurrency to 3 (sandbox limit is 5)
- Worker memory: never arrayBuffer() attachments, always stream
- D1 job_records: INSERT OR IGNORE everywhere for idempotency

## D1 Schema Summary (full schema in packages/db/schema.ts)
users, tenants, tenant_members, org_requests, sf_orgs, sf_tokens,
jobs, job_records, audit_log, billing_events, schedules, sf_cache

## Current Build Status
[ ] monorepo scaffold
[ ] packages/db: Drizzle schema + D1 migrations
[ ] apps/worker: Hono setup + middleware
[ ] SF JWT Bearer token generation
[ ] Web Server OAuth onboarding flow
[ ] Metadata API: deploy custom fields to

## When Writing SF API Code

- Composite API: `{instanceUrl}/services/data/v66.0/composite`
- Metadata API deploy: `{instanceUrl}/services/data/v66.0/metadata/deployRequest`
- All requests: `Authorization: Bearer {accessToken}`
- Sandbox URL: `https://test.salesforce.com`
- Production URL: `https://login.salesforce.com`
- JWT audience must match org type (sandbox vs production)

## Testing

STEP 4 — ALSO CREATE A DECISIONS.md

This prevents Claude Code from second-guessing architectural choices mid-build.

markdown
# Architecture Decisions

## Why Cloudflare over AWS
R2 zero egress, Workers no cold starts, Workflows replace Step Functions,
everything on one platform. Do not suggest AWS services.

## Why JWT Bearer not Web Server refresh tokens for background jobs
Refresh tokens tied to a human user who can leave the company.
JWT Bearer uses our RSA key, runs as dedicated integration user,
regenerated on demand, no stored secrets from client.

## Why Bulk API for Phase 1 and Phase 3
- Phase 1: need all IDs + email bodies for potentially 100k+ records.
  REST API has timeout risk and API limit cost. Bulk is async, unlimited.
- Phase 3: deleting 60k EmailMessages. Bulk handles it in one job.
  Composite API in batches of 25 would be 2,400 API calls.

## Why Queue workers read from R2 CSVs in Phase 2
Phase 2 workers need email body data (HtmlBody, TextBody, all fields).
We already fetched all of this in Phase 1 via Bulk API and stored in R2.
Workers reading from R2 = zero SF API calls for structured data.
Only SF calls remaining: streaming attachment binaries (unavoidable).
This reduces SF API consumption by ~80% vs fetching per-batch.

## Why max_concurrency: 10 on Queue
SF concurrent long-running request limit: 25 (production), 5 (sandbox).
Each worker makes 3-5 SF connections. 10 workers × 5 = 50 connections.
Fast streaming GETs (<20s each) don't count as "long-running" but
we stay conservative to avoid throttling on heavy attachment cases.

## Why allOrNone: false on all Composite calls
Client orgs have validation rules. A single record failing a validation
must not abort the entire batch. Log the failure, continue, report at end.

## Why INSERT OR IGNORE on job_records
Idempotency. If a Queue worker crashes after writing to R2 but before
acking the message, Queue retries the message. Worker re-processes same
Case IDs. INSERT OR IGNORE means D1 state stays correct, R2 PUTs are
idempotent overwrites. Safe to retry at any point.

## Why snapshot Case.Status before restore
SF Flows can trigger on EmailMessage INSERT and change Case.Status.
We store pre-restore Case.Status in snapshot.json. After restore
we check if Status changed and revert. Automatic, no client config needed.

## Why separate admin_setup token vs integration token
Metadata API operations (deploy fields, VF page, button) often require
a real admin user with Customize Application permission. 
Background processing (archive/restore) uses the integration user with
minimal permissions. Keeps least-privilege intact.
